+

\mu_{z}s_{z}

B
unit. triplet

Based on our analysis of particle-hole instabilities, the Dirac cones in Eq. (
45
) can either be those of the non-interacting bands or those of our leading instability at small
D_{0}
—the semimetallic IVC
-
state with band structure shown in Fig.
3
—which could persist for an extended range of
|\nu|>0
; the resulting WZW terms we discuss next are identical in both scenarios.

Due to spin-rotation invariance, triplet pairing is not consistent with a WZW term
Christos et al. 2020
. We find that singlet pairing,
\Delta=\rho_{0}\mu_{0}s_{0}
, which transforms under the irreducible representation
A
of the point group, is the only possible superconducting state with WZW term at
\nu=0
. This is consistent with the singlet we established in Sec.
VII.2
near
\nu=0
due to electron-phonon coupling and particle-hole fluctuations.
Considering all possible compatible insulating orders
m_{j}
, we find there are two types: a spin Hall order of the form
\mu_{z}\rho_{z}s_{x,y,z}
, which aligns with our SSLP
-
order in Table
2
, and a moiré density wave (MDW) state,
\rho_{x}\mu_{x,y}
, which breaks translations on the moiré lattice scale together with an SLP
+
state (
\rho_{z}
), see Table
4
. We note that since this order is defined in the minivalley-valley locked space and, in the full space of MSTG, thus also breaks U(1)
v
symmetry in the same way as our IVC states.
Interestingly, the SSLP
-
is precisely our leading instability for larger
D_{0}
and, hence, constitutes indeed a natural candidate
m_{j}
.
The MDW state goes beyond our analysis in this work, as we have not considered states which break moiré translational symmetry. We will leave this for future work.

At
|\nu|=2
, the situation is more complicated, because the entire band structure is reconstructed as a consequence of interaction-induced flavor polarization. Nonetheless, we expect the low-energy Dirac theory to be still of the form of Eq. (
45
). While there are other possible microscopic realizations, this is true for our leading instability of the SP (or SVP) with additional IVC
-
(or SIVC
-
) order with band structure in Fig.
7
(c,d). Neglecting the small
D_{0}
-induced SP (or SVP) in the graphene-like bands, Eq. (
45
) still applies.

As summarized in Table
4
, all options for insulators with singlet pairing then carry over to
|\nu|=2
. A difference between the two cases is the broken SU(2)
s
spin symmetry, which also allows for triplet pairing. In this case, there are two additional options compatible with the unitary triplet
\Delta=\mu_{z}s_{z}
: one set with insulating orders
\rho_{z}s_{x,y}
and
\rho_{z}\mu_{z}s_{z}
and another set with
\rho_{x}s_{z}\mu_{x,y}
and
\rho_{z}
. The first two orders correspond to our SSLP
+
and SSLP
-
, respectively. The second two states are a spin polarized MDW state and the SLP
+
state. We find no non-unitary state to be consistent with WZW terms.
Since only either singlet or unitary triplet are possible, the underlying flavor polarization of the TBG-like bands in Fig.
3
(c,d) must be SVP rather than SP. Consequently, WZW terms at
|\nu|=2
are more likely relevant if
J_{H}>0
.

VIII
Conclusion and discussion

To summarize, we have studied particle-hole instabilities and superconductivity in MSTG, for different filling fractions
\nu
and displacement field values
D_{0}
, using a combination of analytical arguments and HF numerics. We started, in Sec.
III
, in the limit
D_{0}=0
, where the non-interacting band structure is just given by the spectrum of TBG and single-layer graphene. In the interacting Hamiltonian (
15
), these two subsystems are coupled by the density-density interaction in Eq. (
18
). We showed that states of the form
\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{j}(\nu_{b})}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
, where
\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{j}(\nu_{b})}
are the correlated semimetallic eigenstates of single-layer graphene and
\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
are the eigenstates
Lian et al. 2020
in Eq. (
20
) of the TBG Hamiltonian in the flat limit, are also exact eigenstates of the MSTG in Eq. (
15
). Furthermore, if
\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
are groundstates of the TBG Hamiltonian,
\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{j}(\nu_{b})}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
are shown to be exact groundstates of MSTG for a finite range of the coupling strength, quantified by
\lambda
in Eq. (
18
), between the two sectors. In this sense, not only the bare band structure but also the
interacting
physics of MSTG can be separated into that of single-layer graphene and TBG in the flat-decoupled limit.

We used these results to construct the set of candidate particle-hole orders summarized in Table
2
, which are exactly degenerate in the chiral-flat-decoupled limit [defined by further setting
w_{0}=0
in Eq. (
3
)]. We took these states as our starting point of the HF numerics and analytical perturbation theory, which are not based on
\lambda
being small and allow to tune away from the chiral-flat-decoupled limit.
The resulting band structures for
\nu=0
of the most important candidate orders are shown in Fig.
3
for zero and non-zero
D_{0}
, with
w_{0}
close to what is believed to describe the real system
Nam and Koshino 2017
;
Carr et al. 2019
and finite TBG bandwidth.
We see three distinct types of behavior, with different experimental signatures, when turning on
D_{0}
: the IVC
±
and their Hund’s partners SIVC
±
retain their semimetallic behavior for
D_{0}\neq 0
, while the graphene Dirac cones of the SLP
±
and SSLP
-
(and its Hund’s partner SSLP
+
) are gapped out when turning on
D_{0}
. Interestingly, for the VP and SP (and its Hund’s partner SVP), a finite
D_{0}
induces small Fermi surfaces. In combination with further transport and, in particular, scanning tunneling microscopy experiments, which are sensitive to the local spectrum of the system, the computed spectra could help shed light on the correlated physics of MSTG.

Also the relative energetics between these candidate states as a function of
D_{0}
,
w_{0}
, and the TBG bandwidth is very rich: as summarized in Table
3
, there are many contributions without any analogue in TBG—the mixing between the TBG and graphene bands, additional ordering in the graphene bands, and the
D_{0}
-induced breaking of symmetries in the TBG sector, which changes interaction matrix elements and induces new superexchange processes.
The resultant phase diagram in the
D_{0}
-
w_{0}
plane with leading and subleading phases is presented in Fig.
4
: at small
D_{0}
, a semimetallic intervalley coherent phase is favored, which transitions into a sublattice polarized phase at larger
D_{0}
; among the latter set of states, we expect the SSLP
±
to dominate as they are the only states in this otherwise almost degenerate manifold that can benefit from the intervalley Hund’s coupling
J_{H}
in Eq. (
30
).

At
|\nu|=2
, we found self-consistent HF solutions for all of the candidate states in Table
2
, which coexists with additional spin polarization (either of the SP or SVP type, depending on the sign of
J_{H}
). This can explain the experimentally observed
Park et al. 2021
;
Hao et al. 2021
band resetting for
2\lesssim|\nu|\lesssim 3
. For instance, the spectrum for the intervalley coherent state is shown in Fig.
7
(c) and (d) for
\nu=2
: increasing the filling fraction
\nu
slightly will lead to Fermi surfaces of completely spin-polarized TBG-like bands, i.e., with half the number of flavors.
In magnetic fields, the SP band resetting (
J_{H}<0
) will not change its form, while the SVP-related resetting (
J_{H}>0
) will continuously develop a finite canting and transform into a SP configuration; we estimate the associated magnetic-field scale to be of order of
3\,T
.
Since
J_{H}<0
follows for pure Coulomb interactions
Chatterjee et al. 2020
, we expect the SP to be a more natural candidate; however, also
J_{H}>0
is possible, both theoretically and experimentally, and so we have studied both signs of
J_{H}
in our analysis.

Building on our results for the correlated normal states of MSTG, we analyzed the superconducting order parameters in Sec.
VII
in the different regimes indicated by SC I–III in Fig.
1
: when superconductivity co-exists with flavor polarization (SC I), the nature of the pairing state depends crucially on the form of the flavor reduction; for the SP (
J_{H}<0
) and SVP (
J_{H}>0
) polarization, that we find around
|\nu|=2
, we obtain, respectively, a non-unitary triplet and its Hund’s partner—a singlet-unitary-triplet admixed phase
Scheurer and Samajdar 2020
.
For the superconducting phases (SC II) close to but not in the flavor-polarized region, we find triplet pairing to dominate for
J_{H}<0
, while the state will either be a singlet or admixed singlet-triplet phases for
J_{H}>0
.

The behavior of these superconducting phases in in-plane magnetic fields,
B_{\parallel}
, follows from Ref.
Scheurer and Samajdar 2020
where their respective phase diagrams in the temperature-
B_{\parallel}
-plane have been worked out: in the presence of SP polarization (
J_{H}<0
), the critical temperature,
T_{c}
, of the non-unitary triplet is not affected by the Zeemann coupling and suppressed in quadratic order in the in-plane orbital coupling. This naturally explains the strong violation of the Pauli limit
Cao et al. 2021
.
In the other case of SVP polarization (
J_{H}>0
), the behavior of
T_{c}
of the associated singlet-triplet phase is the same, with the only difference that also the singlet-triplet admixture will gradually transform into a non-unitary triplet with increasing magnetic field.
Understanding the reentrant superconducting behavior seen at even larger magnetic fields
Cao et al. 2021
will require understanding the fate of the correlated parent states, e.g., in Fig.
7
, in large magnetic fields, which we leave for future work.
Irrespective of whether the order parameter of SC II is a triplet or a singlet-triplet admixed phase, it will continuously transform into a non-unitary triplet
Scheurer and Samajdar 2020
upon applying
B_{\parallel}
, while being eventually suppressed by the orbital coupling.
We emphasize that all of the superconducting states we find, including the triplets, are protected
Samajdar and Scheurer 2020
against non-magnetic impurities on the moiré scale, i.e., exhibit an analogue of the “Anderson theorem”, which is typically only expected for singlet superconductors
Mackenzie and Maeno 2003
.

For completeness, we also investigated superconductivity close to the charge neutrality point (SC III), although not prominently seen in current experiments. Here we predict singlet pairing to dominate for the same sign of the Hund’s coupling,
J_{H}>0
, we found triplet pairing near
\nu=2
.

Because the band structure of MSTG also exhibits Dirac cones, a subset of which persist in most of our dominant particle-hole instabilities [see, e.g., IVC
-
in Fig.
3
and Fig.
7
(c,d); related to the preserved
C_{2z}\Theta
symmetry], we have also studied the possible WZW terms between superconducting and insulating orders; as discussed in
Khalaf et al. 2021
;
Christos et al. 2020
for TBG, these topological terms are associated with and sensitive to the chirality of the Dirac cones in the normal-state band structure.
We have shown here that the set of possibilities in MSTG is greatly reduced as compared to TBG
Christos et al. 2020
, resulting from the reduced flavor degeneracy at
|\nu|\gtrsim 2
and the impact of the displacement field at
\nu=0
. As compiled in Table
4
, only
s
-wave singlet pairing is consistent with WZW terms close to
\nu=0
(SC I), while both singlet and triplet pairing can have WZW terms in the flavor-polarized region (SC III). The corresponding particle-hole order parameters that form a mutual WZW term with these superconductors feature the sublattice-polarized states that we find to dominate at finite
D_{0}
(in particular, the SSLP
-
state) and intervalley-coherent moiré density wave (MDW) phases.

Unlike all the states studied in the HF analyses of this paper, the MDW phases break translational symmetry at the scale of the moiré period. The MDW states were
previously studied
Christos et al. 2020
in the context of WZW terms in TBG, break the moiré translational symmetry and the valley U(1)
v
symmetry. A closely related ‘incommensurate Kekulé spiral’ (IKS) appeared in a recent
HF numerics study
Kwan et al. 2021
of TBG. (We also note that Kekulé states have been observed in single layer graphene on a Cu substrate
Gutiérrez et al. 2016
, and in the zeroth Landau level
Li et al. 2019
.) Motivated by these results, and our study of WZW terms here, we believe that these types of states are very promising possible additional instabilities in MSTG as well, in models which include the breaking of the
\text{SU}(2)_{+}\times\text{SU}(2)_{-}
symmetry to the physical SU(2)
s
spin rotation symmetry. We leave a detailed energetic study of these states for future work.

Acknowledgements.

M.S.S. thanks R. Samajdar for discussions and previous collaborations
Scheurer and Samajdar 2020
;
Samajdar and Scheurer 2020
on unconventional superconductivity in moiré systems, and acknowledges discussions with T. Lang, A. Läuchli, and R. Fernandes. This research was supported by the National Science Foundation under Grant No. DMR-2002850. This work was also supported by the Simons Collaboration on Ultra-Quantum Matter, which is a grant from the Simons Foundation (651440, S.S.).

Appendix A
Hamiltonian for the system

Here we will state the detailed form of the interacting Hamiltonian of MSTG used in our work, discuss the relevant symmetries and their representation in the basis employed in this paper, and their consequences for the form factors.

A.1
Continuum model and low-energy degrees of freedom

To describe the non-interacting bands, we use a continuum model, which is just the three-layer version of the continuum model of TBG
Dos Santos et al. 2007
;
Bistritzer and MacDonald 2011
;
Dos Santos et al. 2012
.
Let
c_{\boldsymbol{k};\rho,l,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}}
denote the operator annihilating an electron at crystalline momentum
\boldsymbol{k}
in the moiré Brillouin zone (MBZ), in sublattice
\rho=A,B
and valley
\eta=\pm
of the microscopic graphene sheets, of spin
s=\uparrow,\downarrow
, and with reciprocal lattice (RL) vector
\boldsymbol{G}
of the moiré lattice, thus, forming a triangular lattice;
\boldsymbol{G}=\sum_{j}n_{j}\boldsymbol{G}^{\text{M}}_{j}
,
n_{j}\in\mathbbm{Z}
, with
\boldsymbol{G}_{1}^{\text{M}}=-\sqrt{3}k_{\theta}(1,\sqrt{3})^{T}/2
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{2}^{\text{M}}=\sqrt{3}k_{\theta}(1,0)^{T}
, where
k_{\theta}
represents the magnitude of the vector connecting the K and K
′
points in the MBZ.

Before writing down the Hamiltonian, we follow
Khalaf et al. 2019
and perform a unitary transformation in layer space,

c_{\boldsymbol{k};\rho,l,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}}=V_{l,\ell}\psi_{\boldsymbol{k};\rho,\ell,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}},\qquad V=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&-1\\
0&\sqrt{2}&0\\
1&0&1\end{pmatrix}

(47)

that decomposes the system into mirror-even (
\ell=1,2
) and mirror-odd (
\ell=3
) subspaces (for
D_{0}=0
), as will become apparent below. The full continuum model reads as

H_{0}^{\text{Full}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}=A,B}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1,2,3}\sum_{\eta=\pm}\sum_{s=\uparrow,\downarrow}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}\in\text{RL}}\psi^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};\rho,\ell,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}}\left(h_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}\psi^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}},

(48)

where
h_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=h^{(g)}_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}+h^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}+h^{(D)}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
with the decoupled-layer graphene Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\left(h^{(g)}_{\boldsymbol{k},+}\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{G},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}v_{F}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\theta_{\ell}})_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\left(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{G}-(-1)^{\ell}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}/2\right),

(49)

\displaystyle\left(h^{(g)}_{\boldsymbol{k},-}\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left(h^{(g)}_{-\boldsymbol{k},+}\right)^{*}_{\rho,\ell,-\boldsymbol{G};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},-\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}},

(50)

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\theta}=e^{i\theta\rho_{3}/2}\boldsymbol{\rho}e^{-i\theta\rho_{3}/2}
,
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
connecting the K and K’ points in the MBZ. Throughout the appendix,
\boldsymbol{k}\in
MBZ is measured relative to the M point of the moiré Brillouin zone.
The contribution

\left(h^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{k},+}\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}=\sqrt{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&(T_{\boldsymbol{G}-\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}})_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}&0\\
(T_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{G}}^{*})_{\rho^{\prime},\rho}&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\left(h^{(t)}_{\boldsymbol{k},-}\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}=\left(h^{(t)}_{-\boldsymbol{k},+}\right)^{*}_{\rho,\ell,-\boldsymbol{G};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},-\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}

(51)

to the Hamiltonian describes the tunnelling that is modulated on the moiré lattice. We use the usual BM form
Bistritzer and MacDonald 2011
,

\displaystyle T_{\delta\boldsymbol{G}}=\sum_{j=0,1,2}\delta_{\delta\boldsymbol{G}+\boldsymbol{A}_{j},0}\left[w_{0}\rho_{0}+w_{1}\begin{pmatrix}0&\omega^{j}\\
\omega^{-j}&0\end{pmatrix}\right],

(52)

\displaystyle\omega=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}},\quad\boldsymbol{A}_{0}=0,\quad\boldsymbol{A}_{1}=\boldsymbol{G}_{1},\quad\boldsymbol{A}_{2}=\boldsymbol{G}_{1}+\boldsymbol{G}_{2}.

(53)

Note that it holds
T_{\delta\boldsymbol{G}}^{\dagger}=T_{\delta\boldsymbol{G}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}
and
\rho_{x}T_{\delta\boldsymbol{G}}\rho_{x}=T^{*}_{\delta\boldsymbol{G}}
. So we see very clearly in this basis that the Hamiltonian is given by the sum of the TBG continuum model (with rescaled interlayer couplings) and a single graphene cone. This is different once a displacement field is applied,
D_{0}\neq 0
; its contribution to the Hamiltonian reads as

\left(h^{(D)}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}=-D_{0}\delta_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{G},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&0&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}},

(54)

which is seen to couple the different mirror-eigenvalue sectors, as it breaks the mirror symmetry.

Here and in the following we use the same symbol with subscript
j=0,1,2,3
for Pauli matrices and the associated quantum numbers:
\rho_{j}
are Pauli matrices in sublattice,
s_{j}
in spin, and
\eta_{j}
in valley space.

The Hamiltonian
H_{0}^{\text{Full}}
can be diagonalized by solving the eigenvalue problem

h_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})=\epsilon_{n}(\boldsymbol{k})u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})

(55)

at every
\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}
and defining new fermionic operators
f_{\boldsymbol{k};n,\eta,s}=\sum_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G}}[u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})]_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G}}\psi^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\rho,\ell,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}}
.

In this work, we will mostly focus on the four bands (for each spin and valley flavor) that are closest to the charge-neutrality point. Let us label them by the multi-index
n=(t,p)
with
p=\pm
,
t=g,b
indicating whether these bands are
graphene-like
or
TBG-like
. To make the association unique, we use the conventions

p\,\epsilon_{(t,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})\geq 0,\qquad|\epsilon_{(b,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})|\leq|\epsilon_{(g,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})|.

(56)

In this way, the graphene-like (TBG-like) bands transition to the single-layer graphene (TBG) band structure for
D_{0}\rightarrow 0
. But Eq. (
56
) allows us to generalize this notion to non-zero
D_{0}
.
We will further introduce the notation

b_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}:=f_{\boldsymbol{k};(b,p),\eta,s},\qquad g_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}:=f_{\boldsymbol{k};(g,p),\eta,s}

(57)

on the level of electronic field operators. Our non-interacting, low-energy Hamiltonian then reads as

H_{0}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{\eta=\pm}\sum_{s=\uparrow,\downarrow}\sum_{p=\pm}\left[W_{\text{TBG}}\epsilon_{(b,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})b^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}+\epsilon_{(g,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})g^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}g^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}\right]

(58)

and in total contains
2^{4}=16
bands, associated with valley, spin, Dirac, and even-odd mirror space. In Eq. (
58
), we introduced the dimensionless parameter
W_{\text{TBG}}\in[0,1]
. In the real system, we have
W_{\text{TBG}}=1
and we define
W_{\text{TBG}}=0
as the
flat-band limit
. In Appendix
F.2
below, we will compute the effect of the dispersion to second order in
W_{\text{TBG}}
.

A.2
Single-particle symmetries

As it will play an important role for our analysis of the correlated phases in the system, we will here list the relevant symmetries of the continuum-model Hamiltonian (
48
) and their representation on our low-energy degrees of freedom
b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}
and
g^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}
in Eq. (
58
).

We distinguish four different classes of symmetries:

1.

moiré translations, which we will assume to be preserved throughout and, hence, will not have to be considered explicitly here.

2.

spin and charge conservation symmetries: since we do not take into account spin-orbit coupling nor the for small twist angles suppressed intervalley tunneling processes, the Hamiltonian is invariant under spin and U(1) phase rotations in each valley
\eta=\pm
separately.

3.

point symmetries, forming the point group
C_{6}
(for
D_{0}\neq 0
) or
C_{6h}
(when
D_{0}=0
).

4.

additional internal symmetries: the anti-unitary time-reversal symmetry
\Theta
, the chiral symmetry
C
, and the unitary particle-hole symmetry
P
.

While all other symmetries leave
H_{0}
invariant, the “anti-commuting symmetries”
C
and
P
send
H_{0}\rightarrow-H_{0}
. Note that
C
is only an anti-commuting symmetry if
w_{0}=0
in Eq. (
52
) (but persists for
D_{0}\neq 0
), while
P
is only present if
D_{0}=0
(but persists for
w_{0}\neq 0
). So the removal of the unitary particle hole symmetry in the TBG-like subspace upon turning on
D_{0}
is a novel aspect of the trilayer system as it is always a symmetry in TBG (at least within the analogous continuum-model description).
Among the point symmetries, the mirror symmetry
\sigma_{h}
is only present if
D_{0}=0
, similar to
C_{2x}
in TBG.

In Table
5
, we list the action of point and internal symmetries on the microscopic operators
\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}}
in Eq. (
48
) and on the low-energy operators
b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}
and
g^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}
. The latter representations have to be understood as a gauge-fixing condition; the gauge-fixing conventions we use here, closely follow
Bernevig et al. 2020
. For instance, we choose the phase of the wavefunctions (modulo remaining
\pm
sign) in Eq. (
55
) such that
C_{2z}\Theta u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})=\rho_{1}[u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})]^{*}=u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})
.

To illustrate the gauge fixing and for later reference, we discuss it explicitly for
D_{0}=0
in the mirror-odd sector bands (
\ell=3
). As detailed in Appendix
A.1
, the Hamiltonian in this sector is simply that of single-layer graphene and, in our notation of Eq. (
49
), given by

\left(h^{(e)}_{\boldsymbol{k},+}\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G};\rho^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}=\delta_{\ell,3}\delta_{\ell^{\prime},3}\delta_{\boldsymbol{G},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}v_{F}(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\theta_{\ell}})_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\left(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{G}+\boldsymbol{q}_{1}/2\right)

(59)

in valley
\eta=+
(the wavefunctions in the other valley will simply follow from
C_{2z}
and
\Theta
). Since Eq. (
59
) is already diagonal in the
\boldsymbol{G}
indices, the wavefunctions simply follow from diagonalization of the structure in sublattice space. Focusing, as above, on the lowest bands, we have

\left(u_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{G},-\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}_{1}/2}}\delta_{\ell,3}\frac{i^{(1-p)/2}}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}pe^{-i(\xi_{\delta\boldsymbol{k}}-\theta)/2}\\
e^{i(\xi_{\delta\boldsymbol{k}}-\theta)/2}\end{pmatrix},\quad p=\pm 1,\quad e^{i\xi_{\boldsymbol{k}}}=\frac{k_{x}+ik_{y}}{|\boldsymbol{k}|},\quad\delta\boldsymbol{k}=\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}_{1}/2),

(60)

where we defined the notation
G_{\boldsymbol{k}}
to indicate the reciprocal lattice vector that folds
\boldsymbol{k}
back to the MBZ, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\in\text{MBZ}
. Furthermore, we write
\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}):=\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
for the momentum folded back into the MBZ.

Modulo a (in general
\boldsymbol{k}
and
p
dependent) minus sign to be discussed shortly, the phase of the wavefunctions has been chosen such that (“real gauge”)

C_{2z}\Theta u_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})\equiv\rho_{1}[u_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})]^{*}=u_{n,+}(\boldsymbol{k}).

(61)

The relative minus sign between
p=+
and
p=-
has been fixed in Eq. (
60
) to ensure the behavior,

Cu_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})\equiv-\rho_{3}Cu_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})=-p\,i\,u_{(g,-p),+}(\boldsymbol{k}),

(62)

under
C
given in Table
5
. Note that the relative minus sign at different
\boldsymbol{k}
is simply determined by continuity of
u_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})
as a function of
\boldsymbol{k}
(away from K/K
′
).

Finally, we furthermore fixed the relative signs between wavefunctions at
\boldsymbol{k}
and
\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{G}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{G}_{1}\in\text{RL}
, to be
+1
(“periodic gauge”),

\left(u_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{G}_{1})\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G}}=\left(u_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})\right)_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G}+\boldsymbol{G}_{1}},

(63)

which follows readily from Eq. (
60
) by noting that
\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{G}_{1}}=\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{G}_{1}
, for all
\boldsymbol{G}_{1}\in\text{RL}
.

Table 5

) and in the band basis (

and

). Although redundant, we have added the relevant
\boldsymbol{k}
-local combinations

and

of the symmetries for convenience of the reader. Note that our choice of action of

in the graphene-like subspace is not independent from the other symmetries (it is related to

,

, combined with a valley rotation symmetry). As indicated,

(and, hence,
C_{2z}P
) only anti-commutes with the Hamiltonian if we neglect the rotation of the Pauli matrices
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\theta}
in Eq. (

), which becomes asymptotically valid for small twist angles. As in the main text,

act in valley,

in band,

\eta_{2}\sigma_{2}b_{\boldsymbol{k}}

i\sigma_{2}g_{\boldsymbol{k}}

D_{0}=0
,
\theta\rightarrow 0

With these symmetry representations at hand, it is very simple to see that the band structure of the TBG-like band in Eq. (
58
) has the form,

\epsilon_{(b,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})=p\,E^{b}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k})+\eta\,E^{b}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k})+p\,\eta\,E^{b}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k})+E^{b}_{3}(\boldsymbol{k}),

(64)

where
E^{b}_{1}
(
E^{b}_{2}
) can only be non-zero if the same holds for
w_{0}
(
D_{0}
) such that
C
(
P
) is broken;
E^{b}_{3}\neq 0
requires
w_{0},D_{0}\neq 0
simultaneously. Furthermore, it holds
E^{b}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k})=E^{b}_{j}(-\boldsymbol{k})
, for
j=0,3
, and
E^{b}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k})=-E^{b}_{j}(-\boldsymbol{k})
, for
j=1,2
, due to
\Theta
. Clearly, the band structure for finite
D_{0}
shown in Fig.
2
(b) can only be consistent with Eq. (
64
) if
E^{b}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k})\neq 0
, which is consistent with our observation that
P
must be broken when
D_{0}\neq 0
.

For later reference we introduce the following notation: for any quantity
q(\{x_{k}\})
(such as
E^{b}_{j}
here) that depends on a set of parameters
x_{k}
(here
D_{0}
or
w_{0}
), we introduce
\bar{q}
via

q(\{x_{k}\})=\bar{q}(\{x_{k}\})\,\prod_{k}(x_{k})^{n_{k}},\quad\text{with maximal $n_{k}$ such that we still have}\quad\lim_{x_{k}\rightarrow 0}|\bar{q}(\{x_{k}\})|<\infty.

(65)

This allows us to organize our perturbation theory. For instance, Eq. (
64
) can then be written in the more informative way

\epsilon_{(b,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})=p\,\bar{E}^{b}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k})+\eta\,\bar{E}^{b}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k})\,w_{0}+p\,\eta\,\bar{E}^{b}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k})\,D_{0}+\bar{E}^{b}_{3}(\boldsymbol{k})\,w_{0}D_{0}.

(66)

Similarly, we can analyze the properties of the graphene-like bands. As anticipated above,
P
, when present, does not lead to additional constraints and one readily concludes that

\epsilon_{(g,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})=p\,\bar{E}^{g}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k};\eta)+\bar{E}^{g}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k};\eta)\,w_{0}D_{0},

(67)

where we noticed that the graphene-like bands still have a chiral symmetry for
D_{0}=0
even if
w_{0}\neq 0
.
\Theta
dictates that
\bar{E}^{g}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k};\eta)=\bar{E}^{g}_{j}(-\boldsymbol{k};-\eta)
. Note that, throughout this work (cf. Fig.
2
), we will label the valleys such that
|\epsilon_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})|<|\epsilon_{(g,p),-}(\boldsymbol{k})|
for
\boldsymbol{k}
near the K point.

A.3
Interaction and form factors

We follow a variety of previous works on moiré graphene systems
Xie and MacDonald 2020
;
Bultinck et al. 2019
;
Liu et al. 2021b
;
Liao et al. 2021
;
Bultinck et al. 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Lian et al. 2020
and study a density-density interaction

H_{1}^{\text{Full}}=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q}):\rho^{\text{Full}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\rho^{\text{Full}}_{-\boldsymbol{q}}:,

(68)

with
:...:
indicating normal ordering and

\rho^{\text{Full}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{\rho,\ell,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}}\psi^{\dagger}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q});\rho,\ell,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}}}\psi^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\rho,\ell,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}}

(69)

denoting the Fourier transform of the electron density, where, as above,
\boldsymbol{q}=\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{q})+\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{q}}
with
\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{q})\in\text{MBZ}
. Furthermore,
V(\boldsymbol{q})
in Eq. (
68
) is the momentum-space interaction potential. For our analytical analysis, we will not have to specify the precise form of
V(\boldsymbol{q})
and will only use that
V(\boldsymbol{q})=V(-\boldsymbol{q})>0
. For our numerical computations we use
V(\boldsymbol{q})
as given in Eq. (
9
).

Similar to our discussion of the non-interacting theory, we transform to the eigenbasis of
H_{0}
and only keep the lowest four bands per spin and valley; the resulting projected interaction then reads as

H_{1}=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q}):\rho_{\boldsymbol{q}}\rho_{-\boldsymbol{q}}:,\qquad\rho_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{\eta=\pm}\sum_{s=\uparrow,\downarrow}\sum_{t,t^{\prime}=b,g}\sum_{p,p^{\prime}=\pm}f^{\dagger}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q});(t,p),\eta,s}\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{(t,p),(t^{\prime},p^{\prime})}f^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};(t^{\prime},p^{\prime}),\eta,s},

(70)

where all the microscopic details are encoded in the
form factors

\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{n,n^{\prime}}:=\sum_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G}}\left[u_{n,\eta}(\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}))\right]^{*}_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}}}\left[u_{n^{\prime},\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})\right]_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G}}.

(71)

In order to make sure that the projected interaction respects (many-body) particle-hole symmetry, we follow
Bernevig et al. 2020
and first rewrite the unprojected interaction (
68
) as

\widetilde{H}_{1}^{\text{Full}}=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\delta\rho^{\text{Full}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\delta\rho^{\text{Full}}_{-\boldsymbol{q}}+\dots,\quad\delta\rho^{\text{Full}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{\rho,\ell,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}}\left(\psi^{\dagger}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q});\rho,\ell,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}}}\psi^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\rho,\ell,\eta,s,\boldsymbol{G}}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},0}\right),

(72)

where
\dots
just represent irrelevant constants and chemical potential terms.
The wavefunctions in Eq. (
55
) obey the completeness relation

\sum_{n}[u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})]_{\rho_{1},\ell_{1},\boldsymbol{G}_{1}}[u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})]^{*}_{\rho_{2},\ell_{2},\boldsymbol{G}_{2}}=\delta_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}\delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{G}_{1},\boldsymbol{G}_{2}}.

(73)

Setting
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}=\rho
,
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=\ell
,
\boldsymbol{G}_{1}=\boldsymbol{G}
, and
\boldsymbol{G}_{2}=\boldsymbol{G}+\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}
and summing both sides of Eq. (
73
) over
\rho
,
\ell
, and
\boldsymbol{G}
, we get

\sum_{n}\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}}\right)_{n,n}=\sum_{\rho,\ell,\boldsymbol{G}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{G}^{\prime},0},

(74)

where the sum over
n
is over
all
bands. Using this property we rewrite the full density in Eq. (
72
) as

\delta\rho^{\text{Full}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{n,n^{\prime},\eta,s}\left(f^{\dagger}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q});n,\eta,s}\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{n,n^{\prime}}f^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};n^{\prime},\eta,s}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}\right)_{n,n^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}\right).

(75)

So far, this is still an exact rewriting of the full interaction. However, in this form, the projection of the interaction to the low-energy bands (graphene-like and TBG-like) in a way that respects particle-hole symmetry is particularly natural: one simply has to restrict the sum over
n,n^{\prime}
in Eq. (
75
) to those bands. Defining for notational convenience

\left(F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{((t,p),\eta,s),((t^{\prime},p^{\prime}),\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime})}=\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{(t,p),(t^{\prime},p^{\prime})},

(76)

we then arrive at the final form of the projected interaction

\widetilde{H}_{1}=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\delta\rho_{\boldsymbol{q}}\delta\rho_{-\boldsymbol{q}},\qquad\delta\rho_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\left(f^{\dagger}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q})}F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}f^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}\text{tr}(F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}})\right).

(77)

Let us next discuss properties of these form factors.
While we have computed these form factors numerically using the continuum model, we can make analytical progress by exploiting constraints on them. First, Hermiticity,
\rho_{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}=\rho_{-\boldsymbol{q}}
, implies

\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)^{*}_{n^{\prime},n}=\left(F^{\eta}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}),-\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{n,n^{\prime}}\quad\text{and, specifically,}\quad\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}\right)^{*}_{n^{\prime},n}=\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{G}}\right)_{n,n^{\prime}}.

(78)

Furthermore, since the density operator
\rho^{\text{Full}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}
and, by design, its projection
\rho_{\boldsymbol{q}}
transforms as a scalar function of the vector
\boldsymbol{q}
under all regular and anti-commuting symmetries of
H_{0}
in Table
5
, we obtain further constraints on the form factors. Using all
\boldsymbol{k}
-local operators listed in Table
5
one finds after straightforward algebra:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{(b,p),(b,p^{\prime})}&=\bar{F}^{bb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{0})_{p,p^{\prime}}+i\bar{F}^{bb}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{2})_{p,p^{\prime}}\\
&\quad+\left[\bar{F}^{bb}_{3}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{0})_{p,p^{\prime}}+i\bar{F}^{bb}_{4}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{2})_{p,p^{\prime}}\right]D_{0}^{2}\\
&\quad+\left[\bar{F}^{bb}_{5}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{1})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{bb}_{6}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{3})_{p,p^{\prime}}\right]w_{0}\\
&\quad+\left[\bar{F}^{bb}_{7}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{1})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{bb}_{8}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{3})_{p,p^{\prime}}\right]w_{0}D_{0}^{2},\end{split}

(79a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{(g,p),(g,p^{\prime})}&=\bar{F}^{gg}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{0})_{p,p^{\prime}}+i\bar{F}^{gg}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{2})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{gg}_{3}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{0})_{p,p^{\prime}}+i\bar{F}^{gg}_{4}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{2})_{p,p^{\prime}}\\
&\quad+\left[\bar{F}^{gg}_{5}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{1})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{gg}_{6}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{3})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{gg}_{7}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{1})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{gg}_{8}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{3})_{p,p^{\prime}}\right]w_{0}D_{0}^{2},\end{split}

(79b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{(g,p),(b,p^{\prime})}&=\left[\bar{F}^{gb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{0})_{p,p^{\prime}}+i\bar{F}^{gb}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{2})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{gb}_{3}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{0})_{p,p^{\prime}}+i\bar{F}^{gb}_{4}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{2})_{p,p^{\prime}}\right]D_{0}\\
&\quad+\left[\bar{F}^{gb}_{5}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{1})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{gb}_{6}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta(\sigma_{3})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{gb}_{7}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{1})_{p,p^{\prime}}+\bar{F}^{gb}_{8}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})(\sigma_{3})_{p,p^{\prime}}\right]w_{0}D_{0},\end{split}

(79c)

while
(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}})_{(b,p),(g,p^{\prime})}
follows from Eq. (
78
), i.e.,

\bar{F}^{gb}_{j,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})=\bar{F}^{bg}_{j,\eta}(\text{MBS}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}),-\boldsymbol{q}).

(80)

In Eq. (
79
), all expansion co-efficients are real,
F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\in\mathbb{R}
, and we again employed the convenient notation defined in Eq. (
65
) to make their leading parameter dependence visible. While, by construction, all of the terms in
F^{\eta}_{(g,p),(g,p^{\prime})}
and
F^{\eta}_{(g,p),(b,p^{\prime})}
are absent in the previously studied TBG system, also half of the terms (
F^{bb}_{j}
,
j=3,4,7,8
) entering
F^{\eta}_{(g,p),(g,p^{\prime})}
in Eq. (
79a
) are unique to MSTG, as they can only be non-zero if
D_{0}\neq 0
.

Note that
F^{bb}_{j}
,
j=3,4,7,8
, have to be even functions of
D_{0}
at generic momenta and, hence, start at order
D_{0}^{2}
, which can be seen by noting that
\sigma_{h}
transforms the continuum model Hamiltonian
h_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}(D_{0})
to
h_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}(-D_{0})
; the eigenstates,
u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k};D_{0})
, in Eq. (
55
) thus obey
\sigma_{h}u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k};D_{0})=\pm u_{n,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k};-D_{0})
. Alternatively, it is also clear from the structure of (non-degenerate) perturbation theory in
D_{0}
: corrections to matrix elements of states that have the same eigenvalue under
\sigma_{h}
can only appear in even orders of perturbation theory. The same line of reasoning shows that all
F_{j}^{gb}
have to be odd functions of
D_{0}
, as indicated in Eq. (
79c
).

In addition,
C_{2z}
implies that

\displaystyle\begin{split}F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})&=F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j}(-\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{q}),\qquad j=1,2,7,8,\\
F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})&=-F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j}(-\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{q}),\qquad j=3,4,5,6.\end{split}

(81)

The Hermiticity constraint in Eq. (
78
) further implies for the form factors with
t=t^{\prime}
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}F^{tt}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})&=F^{tt}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{G}),\qquad j=1,3,5,6,7,8,\\
F^{tt}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})&=-F^{tt}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{G}),\qquad j=2,4.\end{split}

(82)

For later reference, we combine this with Eq. (
81
) above to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}F^{tt}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})&=F^{tt}_{j}(-\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}),\qquad j=1,4,7,8\\
F^{tt}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})&=-F^{tt}_{j}(-\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}),\qquad j=2,3,5,6.\end{split}

(83)

Although we will not use
C_{3z}
constraints on the form factors explicitly in our analytical considerations, we note that it further forces the momentum dependence of all pre-factors to obey
F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})=F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j}(C_{3z}\boldsymbol{k},C_{3z}\boldsymbol{q})
.

Finally, we point out that
F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}=0}=\mathbbm{1}
, for all
\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}
, as readily follows from the definition (
71
). As such, we have

F_{j}^{tt^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}=0)=\delta_{j,1}\delta_{t,t^{\prime}}.

(84)

The form factors of the mirror-odd (graphene) subsystem for
D_{0}=0
can be readily computed from the wavefunctions defined in Eq. (
60
). One finds

\left(F^{+}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{(g,p),(g,p^{\prime})}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}_{1}}{2}},\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{k}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}_{1}}{2}}}\left[\delta_{p,p^{\prime}}\cos\left(\frac{\xi_{\delta(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q})}-\xi_{\delta\boldsymbol{k}}}{2}\right)+i(\sigma_{y})_{p,p^{\prime}}\sin\left(\frac{\xi_{\delta(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q})}-\xi_{\delta\boldsymbol{k}}}{2}\right)\right].

(85)

Note that this agrees with the symmetry-based structure given in Eq. (
79b
). On top of this, we observe that the matrix elements obey

\left(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}\right)_{(g,p),(g,p^{\prime})}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{G},0}\delta_{p,p^{\prime}}.

(86)

We will use both the explicit form in Eq. (
85
) as well as the special property (
86
) of the graphene matrix elements below.

Appendix B
Exact statements about the interacting groundstates

In this appendix, we will discuss limits where exact many-body groundstates of MSTG can be derived. To this end, we will focus on
D_{0}=0
, where the
non-interacting
part of the Hamiltonian,
H_{0}
, is just given by the sum of the TBG continuum model,
H_{0}^{\text{b}}
, and that of single-layer graphene,
H_{0}^{\text{g}}
. Importantly, the presence of mirror symmetry
\sigma_{h}
also leads to
F^{gb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})=F^{bg}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})=0
, see Eq. (
79c
). Consequently, the density operator
\rho_{\boldsymbol{q}}
in Eq. (
70
) and its symmetrized cousin
\delta\rho_{\boldsymbol{q}}
in Eq. (
77
) are just given by the sum of the contributions from the graphene (g) and TBG (b) subsystem. Focusing on
\delta\rho
, we have
\delta\rho_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\delta\rho^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}+\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}

with

\displaystyle\delta\rho^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\left(\sum_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}g^{\dagger}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q});\mu}\left(F^{gg}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}g^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\mu^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}\text{tr}(F^{gg}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}})\right),

(87)

\displaystyle\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\left(\sum_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}b^{\dagger}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q});\mu}\left(F^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right)_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\mu^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}\text{tr}(F^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}})\right),

(88)

where we introduced the multi-indices comprising band, valley, and spin,
\mu=(p,\eta,s)
,
and the submatrix form factors in the respective subsystem,
(F^{tt}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}})_{(p,\eta,s),(p^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime})}:=\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}(F^{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}})_{(t,p),(t,p^{\prime})}
. Notwithstanding this simplification, the interaction in Eq. (
77
) still couples the graphene and TBG subsystem and it is not clear how this will affect the groundstates.
In particular, it is unclear whether one can still write down exact eigenstates (or even groundstates) of the combined system, as has been done in TBG
Lian et al. 2020
, and whether the system will also exhibit order in the graphene subspace.

B.1
Turning on the coupling between the subsystems

To address these questions, we split the projected Hamiltonian of the system into three parts,

H_{0}+\widetilde{H}_{1}=H^{\text{g}}+H^{\text{b}}+\lambda H^{\text{gb}},

(89)

where

H^{\text{g}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{\eta,p=\pm}\sum_{s=\uparrow,\downarrow}pE_{0}^{g}(\boldsymbol{k};\eta)g^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}g^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}+\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\delta\rho^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\delta\rho^{\text{g}}_{-\boldsymbol{q}}

(90)

is just the usual Hamiltonian of graphene with Coulomb interaction, while

H^{\text{b}}=W_{\text{TBG}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{\eta,p=\pm}\sum_{s=\uparrow,\downarrow}(p\,\bar{E}^{b}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k})+\eta\,\bar{E}^{b}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k})\,w_{0})b^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}+\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{-\boldsymbol{q}}

(91)

is the TBG Hamiltonian, consisting of the quasi-flat bands interacting via a projected density-density interaction. We further introduced the dimensionless parameter
\lambda
that allows us to adiabatically turn on the density-density interaction,

\lambda H^{\text{gb}}=\frac{\lambda}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\delta\rho^{\text{g}}_{-\boldsymbol{q}}\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}

(92)

between the two subsystems, with
\lambda=1
corresponding to the physical system.

Let us start with
\lambda=0
, where the Hamiltonian is given by
H^{\text{g}}+H^{\text{b}}
. Being the sum of the two commuting subsystem Hamiltonians, its eigenstates are just given by all combinations of the individual eigenstates of graphene and TBG. As discussed in more detail in the main text, the ground state of the graphene subspace is a symmetry-unbroken correlated semimetal. We denote its ground state by
\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{0}(\nu_{b})}
and its (gapless) excited states by
\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{j}(\nu_{b})}
,
j>0
, where
\nu_{b}
indicates that the filling fraction of the graphene sector is a function of the filling
\nu_{b}
in the TBG sector. We will here focus on
\nu_{\text{b}}=0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3,\pm 4
.

The exact ground states of certain limits of
H^{\text{b}}
in Eq. (
91
) at integer filling fractions
\nu_{b}
have been discussed in detail in
Lian et al. 2020
and we next use these insights, within our notation, and transfer them to MSTG. First, one can see from Eq. (
79a
) that the TBG form factors become

\bar{F}^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}=\bar{F}^{bb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\sigma_{0}+i\bar{F}^{bb}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\sigma_{2}

(93)

in the chiral-decoupled limit (
w_{0}=D_{0}=0
). We can, hence, bring the form factors and density operators to a diagonal form by introducing another set of field-operators,

\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{\eta,c=\pm,s}\bar{F}^{bb}_{c}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\left(\tilde{b}^{\dagger}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q});c,\eta,s}\tilde{b}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};c,\eta,s}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}\right),\,\tilde{b}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};c,\eta,s}=U_{c,p}b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s},\quad U=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-i\\
1&i\end{pmatrix},

(94)

where
\bar{F}^{bb}_{\pm}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})=\bar{F}^{bb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\pm i\bar{F}^{bb}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})
.
As is readily verified, the following set of states
Lian et al. 2020

\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}=\prod_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\prod_{c=\pm}\prod_{j_{c}=1}^{\nu_{c}}\tilde{b}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};c,\eta^{c}_{j_{c}},s^{c}_{j_{c}}}\ket{0^{\text{b}}}

(95)

with
\nu_{+}+\nu_{-}=\nu_{\text{b}}
obey

\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}R_{\boldsymbol{G}}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})},\qquad R_{\boldsymbol{G}}=\nu_{b}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}F_{1}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}),

(96)

in the chiral limit,
w_{0}=0
, for arbitrary combinations of occupied spin/valley-flavors
\{\eta^{\pm}_{j},s^{\pm}_{j}\}
. Consequently, all of these states are eigenstates of
H^{\text{b}}
in the flat-limit, i.e., when
W_{\text{TBG}}=0
(or right at the magic angle for
w_{0}=0
where the bands become perfectly flat). It can further be shown
Lian et al. 2020
that all
\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
in Eq. (
95
) are groundstates of
H^{\text{b}}
for
\nu_{b}=0
and that the same holds for all integer
\nu_{b}\neq 0
, if the flat-metric condition,

F^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}=\mathbbm{1}f(\boldsymbol{G}),\qquad\forall\,\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}

(97)

applies. As argued in Ref.
Lian et al. 2020
, Eq. (
97
) holds approximately for realistic parameters of TBG and the
\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
in Eq. (
95
) will remain
exact
ground states if it is only weakly violated. The fact that there are several exact ground states is associated with an emergent enhanced continuous symmetry [
(\text{U}(4)\times\text{U}(4))_{\text{b,cf}}
] in the chiral-flat limit
Bultinck et al. 2019
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
;
Bernevig et al. 2020
.

Once
w_{0}\neq 0
, i.e., in the nonchiral-flat limit, this symmetry group is reduced [to U(4)] and only the subset of states in Eq. (
95
) where
\eta_{j}^{+}=\eta_{j}^{-}
and
s_{j}^{+}=s_{j}^{-}
, which can only be defined for even
\nu_{b}
, are exact eigenstates of
\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}
and obey Eq. (
96
). They are always the exact ground states at
\nu_{b}=0
, while this only holds if Eq. (
97
) is not sufficiently violated for
\nu_{b}=\pm 2
. We refer to Ref.
Lian et al. 2020
for the derivations of these statements.

Having established the properties of the spectrum of the graphene subsystem and the exact groundstates of the TBG Hamiltonian,
H^{\text{b}}
, we can next address what happens when the coupling
\lambda H^{\text{gb}}
is turned on. As a first step, we rewrite the coupling Hamiltonian in Eq. (
92
) as

H^{\text{gb}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[\delta\rho^{\text{g}}_{-\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}R_{\boldsymbol{G}}\right)+\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}R_{\boldsymbol{G}}\delta\rho^{\text{g}}_{-\boldsymbol{G}}\right].

(98)

Recall from Eq. (
86
) that
F^{gg}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{G},0}\mathbbm{1}
, i.e., the graphene form factors obey the flat-metric condition (
97
) exactly (with
f(\boldsymbol{G})=\delta_{\boldsymbol{G},0}
) in the limit
D_{0}=0
, we have

\delta\rho^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}=N\delta_{\boldsymbol{G},0}\hat{\nu}_{g},\qquad\hat{\nu}_{g}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sum_{\mu}\left(g^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};\mu}g^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\right)

(99)

and

H^{\text{gb}}=H^{\text{gb},0}+V(0)\hat{\nu}_{g},\qquad H^{\text{gb},0}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\delta\rho^{\text{g}}_{-\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}R_{\boldsymbol{G}}\right).

(100)

Note that the last term proportional to the filling fraction
\hat{\nu}_{g}
of the graphene subspace is only a constant in the low-energy subspace of the system since the TBG ground states in Eq. (
95
) are known to be insulators and, hence, exhibit a charge gap (in fact, also the two-particle spectrum was shown to be gapped
Bernevig et al. 2020
; more generally, this is also expected based on the experimental absence of superconductivity at integer filling in TBG in the vicinity of the magic angle, which implies that potential additional two-particle bound states cannot occur below the gapped particle-hole continuum).

Using the form of the coupling in Eq. (
100
) and recalling that the exact groundstates of the TBG sector obey Eq. (
96
), we immediately see that

H^{\text{gb},0}\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{j}}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}=0

(101)

for both the graphene ground state,
j=0
, and its excited states,
j>0
, and all exact TBG groundstates discussed above. As such, all of these states remain
exact
eigenstates for arbitrary
\lambda
.

Next, we address the question whether they remain the ground states for a finite range of
\lambda>0
. First, as we have already discussed, the TBG sector has a finite charge gap and, hence, states which involve wavefunctions where one (or more) electrons of the TBG sector are transferred to the graphene sector can only become ground states at sufficiently large
\lambda
.
Second, since the TBG ground states break symmetries, there are gapless Goldstone modes which require a more careful analysis. This is presented in the next subsection.

B.2
Behavior of Goldstone modes

As follows from
C_{3z}
rotational symmetry (and has been demonstrated in Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2020
), the Goldstone spectrum of TBG must be of the form
E_{\text{G}}(\boldsymbol{p})=\frac{1}{2}\rho\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{p}^{4})
, with (isotropic) stiffness
\rho>0
.
Due to the arbitrarily small energies of the TBG Goldstone modes for
\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow 0
at
\lambda=0
, we have to make sure that non-zero
\lambda
will not immediately lower the energy of these states below that of
\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{0}}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
in MSTG. For that reason, we have to compute the evolution of the
\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow 0
Goldstone modes of the TBG sector upon turning on
\lambda
. Using momentum
\boldsymbol{p}
to label those states, we have to compute the behavior of their energy
\Delta E(\lambda,\boldsymbol{p})
(relative to
\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{0}}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
). Assuming that
\Delta E(\lambda,\boldsymbol{p})
is analytic in
\boldsymbol{p}
,
C_{3z}
again implies that

\Delta E(\lambda,\boldsymbol{p})=\frac{1}{2}\rho\,\boldsymbol{p}^{2}+m(\lambda)+\delta\rho(\lambda)\boldsymbol{p}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{p}^{4})

(102)

and our goal will be to determine
m(\lambda)
and
\delta\rho(\lambda)
.

To get started, let us investigate the wavefunctions of the Goldstone modes of TBG for
\lambda=0
. Fortunately, these can be computed exactly in the chiral-flat limit
Bernevig et al. 2020
, as the property in Eq. (
96
) reduces their computation to a one-particle problem. The mathematical reason for this is that
[H^{\text{b}},\tilde{b}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{p};c_{2},\eta_{2},s_{2}}\tilde{b}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};c_{1},\eta_{1},s_{1}}]\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
is a superposition of states of the form
\tilde{b}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{p};c_{a},\eta_{a},s_{a}}\tilde{b}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime};c_{b},\eta_{b},s_{b}}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
. Further noting that the density operator in Eq. (
94
) does not scatter states between different quantum numbers
c
,
\eta
,
s
and is independent of
\eta
,
s
, we see that the Goldstone modes with momentum
\boldsymbol{p}
must be of the form

\ket{\Psi_{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b};c_{1},\eta_{1},s_{1};c_{2},\eta_{2},s_{2})}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\alpha_{\boldsymbol{p},c_{1},c_{2}}(\boldsymbol{k})\tilde{b}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{p};c_{2},\eta_{2},s_{2}}\tilde{b}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};c_{1},\eta_{1},s_{1}}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})},

(103)

where the
(c_{1},\eta_{1},s_{1})
flavor is occupied and
(c_{2},\eta_{2},s_{2})
is unoccupied in the specific groundstate
\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}
we start from.
In the limit
\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow 0
, Ref.
Bernevig et al. 2020
has shown that one Goldstone zero mode has
c_{1}=c_{2}
and
\alpha_{\boldsymbol{p}=0,c,c}(\boldsymbol{k})=\alpha
, i.e.,

\ket{\Psi_{\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow 0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b};c,\eta_{1},s_{1};c,\eta_{2},s_{2})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\tilde{b}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};c,\eta_{2},s_{2}}\tilde{b}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};c,\eta_{1},s_{1}}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}.

(104)

All other zero-energy Goldstone modes follow from
(\text{U}(4)\times\text{U}(4))_{\text{b,cf}}
rotations.

It is a matter of straightforward algebra to show that

\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\ket{\Psi_{\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow 0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b};c,\eta_{1},s_{1};c,\eta_{2},s_{2})}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}R_{\boldsymbol{G}}\ket{\Psi_{\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow 0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b};c,\eta_{1},s_{1};c,\eta_{2},s_{2})},\quad R_{\boldsymbol{G}}=\nu_{b}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}F_{1}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})\in\mathbbm{R}.

(105)

Since
\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}
is invariant under
(\text{U}(4)\times\text{U}(4))_{\text{b,cf}}
rotations, this also holds for all other zero-energy Goldstone modes. In fact, this behavior can be understood more generally and intuitively by rewriting the
H_{b}
in Eq. (
91
) (recall we always focus on the flat-band limit,
W_{\text{TBG}}=0
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}H^{\text{b}}&=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left(\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}R_{\boldsymbol{G}}\right)\left(\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{-\boldsymbol{q}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{-\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}R_{\boldsymbol{G}}\right)\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}V(\boldsymbol{G})R_{\boldsymbol{G}}\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}V(\boldsymbol{G})R_{\boldsymbol{G}}R_{-\boldsymbol{G}}.\end{split}

(106)

While the last term in Eq. (
106
) is just a constant, it holds

\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{G}}=Nf(\boldsymbol{G})\hat{\nu}_{b},\qquad\hat{\nu}_{b}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sum_{\mu}\left(b^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};\mu}b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\right),

(107)

if the flat-metric condition (
97
) applies. Since
R_{\boldsymbol{G}}=R_{-\boldsymbol{G}}
, following from Eq. (
82
), and as
\nu_{b}
is constant in the low-energy subspace of TBG, we can write in this subspace
Kang and Vafek 2019

H^{\text{b}}=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\mathcal{O}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}+\text{const.},\qquad\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{q}}:=\sqrt{V(\boldsymbol{q})}\left(\delta\rho^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{G}}R_{\boldsymbol{G}}\right).

(108)

Since all exact TBG groundstates discussed above obey Eq. (
96
), or
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\ket{\Psi_{0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b})}=0
, all Goldstone modes with vanishing momentum,
\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow 0
, must be annihilated by all
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{q}}
as their energy must approach that of the groundstates. This is why Eq. (
105
) must hold. Note that, for
\nu_{b}=0
, we did not have to assume the flat-metric condition since
R_{\boldsymbol{G}}=0
.

Having established Eq. (
105
), we also immediately see that

H^{\text{gb},0}\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{j}}\ket{\Psi_{\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow 0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b};c_{1},\eta_{1},s_{1};c_{2},\eta_{2},s_{2})}=0.

(109)

This shows that also
\ket{\Psi^{\text{g}}_{j}}\ket{\Psi_{\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow 0}^{\text{b}}(\nu_{b};c_{1},\eta_{1},s_{1};c_{2},\eta_{2},s_{2})}
remain exact eigenstates of the Hamiltonian for any
\lambda\neq 0
and that
m(\lambda)=0
in Eq. (
102
). Making the natural assumption that
\delta\rho(\lambda)
is analytic in
\lambda
, we can expand

\delta\rho(\lambda)\sim\sum_{n\geq 0}c^{\rho}_{n}\lambda^{n}.

(110)

Therefore, it will require a finite value of
\lambda
before
\Delta E(\lambda,\boldsymbol{p})
can lose its positive semi-definite nature and the groundstates discussed above have to remain the groundstates in a finite region of
\lambda>0
.

As we discuss in Sec.
VIII
of the main text, one possibility is that at sufficiently large
\lambda
a state with finite momentum
\boldsymbol{p}
resulting from a mixing between the graphene and TBG degrees of freedom becomes the ground state. We leave a quantitative analysis of such a MDW state
Christos et al. 2020
for future work.

Appendix C
Hartree-Fock functional

C.1
General form

Let us assume that the ground state is the Slater determinant
\ket{\psi[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]}
characterized by the correlator

(P_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}=\braket{\psi[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]|f^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};\alpha}f^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\alpha^{\prime}}|\psi[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]},\qquad P_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}=P^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=(P^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})^{2},

(111)

where we use the multi-index
\alpha=((t,p),\eta,s)
to keep the expressions more compact. The HF mean-field Hamiltonian
H^{\text{MF}}
associated with
H_{0}+H_{1}
, defined in Eqs. (
58
) and (
70
) contains four terms,

H^{\text{MF}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=H^{\text{MF}}_{k}+H^{\text{MF}}_{\text{H}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]+H^{\text{MF}}_{\text{F}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]+E_{0}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}].

(112)

Postponing the definition of the energetic off-set,
E_{0}
, which is proportional to the identity operator, these terms are the kinetic term

H^{\text{MF}}_{k}=H_{0}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}f^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};\alpha}h^{k}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{k})f^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};\alpha^{\prime}}\equiv\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}f^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})f^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k}},

(113)

where
h^{k}
is the diagonal matrix

h^{k}_{((t,p),\eta,s),((t^{\prime},p^{\prime}),\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime})}(\boldsymbol{k})=\delta_{t,t^{\prime}}\delta_{p,p^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\epsilon_{(t,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})

(114)

with
\epsilon_{(t,p),\eta}
given in Eqs. (
66
) and (
67
), the Hartree term,

H^{\text{MF}}_{\text{H}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}V(\boldsymbol{G})\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{G}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}f_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}f_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\phantom{\dagger}},\qquad\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{G}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\text{tr}\left[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}F^{*}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}\right],

(115)

and the Fock contribution

H^{\text{MF}}_{\text{F}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}f_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}F^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}P^{T}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q})}F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}f_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}.

(116)

The associated ground state energy can be decomposed into three contributions,

E^{\text{HF}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=\braket{\psi[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]|(H_{0}+H_{1})|\psi[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]}=E_{\text{k}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]+E_{\text{H}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]+E_{\text{F}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}],

(117a)

which read as

\displaystyle E_{\text{k}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\text{tr}\left[P^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})\right],

(117b)

\displaystyle E_{\text{H}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}V(\boldsymbol{G})\left|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\text{tr}\left[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}F^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}\right]\right|^{2},

(117c)

\displaystyle E_{\text{F}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\text{tr}\left[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}F^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}P_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q})}F^{*}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\right].

(117d)

Having established this notation, the energetic off-set
E_{0}
in Eq. (
112
) can now be conveniently stated as
E_{0}=-E_{\text{H}}-E_{\text{F}}
.

As has been discussed in previous works on TBG
Bultinck et al. 2019
;
Liu et al. 2021b
;
Xie and MacDonald 2020
;
Liao et al. 2021
, it is important to note that the continuum model defined in Sec.
A.1
already takes into account some correlation effects of the system. So using the continuum model dispersion in
h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})
in the HF mean-field Hamiltonian as defined in Eq. (
112
) would constitute a double counting of these effects. To avoid this, we replace (ignoring the irrelevant constant
E_{0}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{0}])

H^{\text{MF}}_{k}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]\quad\longrightarrow\quad H^{\text{MF}}_{k}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]-\left(H^{\text{MF}}_{\text{H}}[P^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}]+H^{\text{MF}}_{\text{F}}[P^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}]\right)

(118)

in Eq. (
112
), where
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{0}
is a reference density matrix for which the continuum model dispersion is expected to be valid. The redefinition in Eq. (
118
) is equivalent to replacing

h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})\quad\longrightarrow\quad\widetilde{h}^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})=h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})-h_{\text{HF}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{0}](\boldsymbol{k}),

(119)

in Eq. (
113
), where we defined
h_{\text{HF}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]
such that
H^{\text{MF}}_{\text{H}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]+H^{\text{MF}}_{\text{F}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}f_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}h_{\text{HF}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}](\boldsymbol{k})f_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}
.

To connect to our analytical discussion and with other work on TBG
Bernevig et al. 2020
;
Lian et al. 2020
;
Bernevig et al. 2020
, we will use a subtraction point such that the starting Hamiltonian of our HF analysis is equal to (modulo a constant) the non-normal ordered but manifestly particle-hole symmetric Hamiltonian
H=H_{0}+\widetilde{H}_{1}
with
\widetilde{H}_{1}
given in Eq. (
77
).
It is a matter of straightforward algebra to show that this corresponds to using
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{0}=\mathbbm{1}/2
in Eq. (
119
).

C.2
Numerical procedure

C.2.1
How we fix the phases in numerics

In this appendix, we describe how we fix the phases of the wavefunctions in the TBG sector in our numerics, using symmetries. As above, we denote the wavefunction in valley
\eta=\pm
, at momentum
\boldsymbol{k}
, and of band
n=\pm
by
u_{n,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}
; these are vectors in layer, sublattice, and
\boldsymbol{G}
space.

To fix the phase of the wavefunctions (modulo
\pm
) at every
\boldsymbol{k}
point,
\eta
, and
n
, we use that the Hamiltonian commutes with
C_{2z}\Theta
and enforce that

C_{2z}\Theta u_{n,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}=u_{n,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}.

(120)

All that is left to do is remove the relevant parts of the remaining
\boldsymbol{k}
,
\eta
, and
n
dependent sign ambiguity.
As a first step, we fix the relative sign of
u_{n,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}
and
u_{n,-\eta,-\boldsymbol{k}}
by choosing

\Theta u_{n,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}=u_{n,-\eta,-\boldsymbol{k}}.

(121)

Because of Eq. (
120
), this is equivalent to
C_{2z}u_{n,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}=u_{n,-\eta,-\boldsymbol{k}}
.

Next we fix the relative sign of the wavefunctions in the two bands
n=+
and
n=-
at each
\boldsymbol{k}
and
\eta
. If we are in the chiral limit and the system has the chiral symmetry
C
(which anti-commutes with the Hamiltonian at each
\boldsymbol{k}
), we can just enforce

Cu_{\pm,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}=\pm i\eta\,u_{\mp,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}.

(122)

Note it is easy to see that the prefactor has to have the form
\pm i\eta
since
C^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
,
\{C_{2z}\Theta,C\}=0
, and
[\Theta,C]=0
. In our numerics, we can readily implement Eq. (
122
) by

u_{+,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rightarrow\,u_{+,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}},\qquad u_{-,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rightarrow\,-i\,\eta\braket{u_{-,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}|C|u_{+,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}}u_{-,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}

(123)

at every
\boldsymbol{k}
point.

When
w_{0}\neq 0
but not extremely large, we can expect
Cu_{\pm,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}\simeq\text{const.}\times u_{\mp,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}
. So we replace Eq. (
123
) by the generalized condition

u_{+,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rightarrow\,u_{+,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}},\qquad u_{-,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rightarrow\,\eta\,\text{sign}\left[\text{Im}\braket{u_{-,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}|C|u_{+,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}}\right]u_{-,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}.

(124)

Note that Eq. (
120
) actually implies
\braket{u_{-,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}|C|u_{+,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}}\in i\mathbbm{R}
even away from the chiral limit.

Finally, the only remaining relevant relative sign is that of
u_{n,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}
and
u_{n,-\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}
. We fix this one by making sure that

C_{2z}Pu_{n,\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}=n\,\eta\,u_{-n,-\eta,\boldsymbol{k}}.

(125)

In practice, we make sure that Eq. (
125
) holds by adjusting the sign of the wavefunctions of
both
bands in the
\eta=-
valley according to

u_{+,+,\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rightarrow\,u_{+,+,\boldsymbol{k}}\bra{u_{++,\boldsymbol{k}}}C_{2z}P\ket{u_{--,\boldsymbol{k}}}\qquad u_{+,-,\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rightarrow\,-\braket{u_{-,+,\boldsymbol{k}}|C_{2z}P|u_{+,-,\boldsymbol{k}}}u_{+,-,\boldsymbol{k}},\qquad n=\pm.

(126)

C.2.2
Iterative procedure

We will here give the details of how we numerically solve the HF equations in MSTG and find self-consistent solutions
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}
in Eq. (
111
). Our iterative procedure has the following steps:

1.

Guess an initial form of the projector
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}
. We choose the initial ansatz for
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}
for a given symmetry breaking state to be those given in the band basis in Table
3
.

2.

P_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is then substituted into the HF functional expression (
31
). The HF functional is then diagonalized at each point via a unitary transformation
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
.

3.

P_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is then recomputed from the HF functional as
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}=U_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{*}DU^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
where
D
is the density matrix in the diagonal basis at zero temperature, with 1’s on the diagonal elements corresponding to filled bands of the HF Hamiltonian and zeros elsewhere.

4.

We then check if
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}
has converged from the previous iteration. If it has, the procedure is finished and we have found a self-consistent solution characterized by
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}
. If
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}
has not converged, we return to step 2.

Unless otherwise specified, we include 3 shells of moiré Brillouin zones in our construction of the trilayer model (37 unit moiré unit cells total) of which we include out to 2 shells of moiré Brillouin zones in our numerical calculations and a 10
\times
10 grid of
\boldsymbol{k}
-points per moiré Brillouin zone (243
\boldsymbol{k}
-points total per moiré Brillouin zone). Unless otherwise specified, we take
\epsilon=7
and screening length
d_{s}=40
nm.

C.2.3
Ground State dependence on grid size

Here, we make note of an effect we observe for smaller grid size for a 6
\times
6
\boldsymbol{k}
grid (75
\boldsymbol{k}
-points per moiré unit cell). We find for the smaller grid, a region of spin or valley polarized ground state emerges for intermediate, nonzero
D_{0}
and intermediate to large
w_{0}
which became disfavored relative to the IVC
-
state as we increased the grid size to better resolve the Dirac cones of the graphene-like bands. We attribute this difference to momentum dependent mixing between remote bands in the Fock term.

C.3
Varying Parameters

In Fig.
8
, we show the band structures obtained from our self-consistent calculation for all ansätze we attempt, exlcluding the spin Hall state which is quantitatively and qualitatively similar to our SLP
-
state which is shown. The contour taken through the moiré Brillouin zone to produce the band structures is shown in Fig.
2
.

Figure 8
:
We show band structures of converged solutions at
\nu=0
corresponding to the ansätze in Table
3
, including those which we do not appear in the phase diagram for dielectric constant
\epsilon=7
at zero and nonzero
D_{0}
.

We verify our results are relatively independent of parameters may vary in experiment. We first consider additional values of the dielectric constant
\epsilon
, which controls the relative contributions to the Hartree-Fock energy of the kinetic and interaction terms. We find the phase boundary between the IVC
-
state and sublattice polarized states shifts slightly but the overall features do not depend on the values of
\epsilon
we test in Fig.
9
.

Figure 9
:
Phases as we vary the dielectric constant in the Coulomb potential in Eq. (
9
) from
\epsilon=4
(left),
\epsilon=7
(center), and
\epsilon=15
(right). As the
\epsilon
is varied, the phase boundaries shift but the presences of a time-reversal-odd IVC phase and sublattice polarized phase are robust against changes in the potential. As
\epsilon
increases, the bandwidth of the TBG-like bands relative to the Coulomb potential
V(\boldsymbol{q})
increases; as predicted in Table
3
, the
D_{0}
-dependent increases in bandwidth favors a sublattice polarized state over the IVC
-
.

We also vary the screening length
d_{s}
from 10 nm to 80 nm and find while the relative energies shift, the phase diagram does not change when
d_{s}
is varied. We also verify our results are stable to increasing the number of bands we keep in our self-consistent calculation, by repeating the same calculation with the closest 8 bands per valley and spin to the Fermi level at charge neutrality. The resulting band structures as
D_{0}
is varied are shown in Fig.
11
. We note the IVC
+
which we found a converged solution for in the calculation keeping only 4 bands per spin and valley no longer converges when more bands are added and is not shown in Fig.
11
. Given the large energy difference separating the IVC
+
from the other lower energy symmetry breaking states, the absence of the IVC
+
on doubling the number of bands is not relevant to our main results. We also increase our
\boldsymbol{k}
-grid size up to a 14
\times
14 grid to verify convergence.

Figure 10
:
We show band structures for a HF calculation with a 14
\times
14 grid for extreme value
\epsilon=15
,
d_{s}=40
nm.

Figure 11
:
We show band structures for a self-consistent calculation keeping 8 rather than 4 bands per spin-valley flavor, for
\epsilon=7
,
d_{s}=40
nm.

Appendix D
Numerics at
\nu=2

D.1
Stable Phases at
\nu=2

In this appendix we will briefly discuss our numerical results at
\nu=2
. We readjust our numerical procedure such that the chemical potential is recomputed at each iteration to fix the filling at
\nu=+2
electrons per moiré unit cell. In the projected space of the TBG-like and graphene-like bands, this means the trace of
Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}
satisfies
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\text{Tr}[Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=10
. We also adjust the projectors
P
corresponding to our starting ansätze to be compatible with half-filling by setting
P_{b,\nu=2}=\frac{1}{2}(1+s_{z})P_{b,\nu=0}
in the TBG-bands at
\nu=2
. The structure of
P_{g,\nu=2}=\frac{1}{2}(1-\sigma_{z})
is the same as at charge neutrality. The energies resulting from our HF calculations are shown in Fig.
12
. We observe no qualitative change from the phases at charge neutrality, but note the spin-polarized IVC
-
and spin-valley polarized state are closer in energy at
\nu=2
. We show the band structures obtained for the full set of ansätze we check in Fig.
13
. We note that where the IVC
-
at charge neutrality was a semimetal, at
\nu=2
the spin polarized IVC
-
is a metal with a small Fermi surface. We also note that the spin polarized versions of the sublattice polarized states now are either a metal or semimetal. The change of the SLP states to semimetals at
|\nu|=2
can be understood as state with an insulating SLP order in the TBG-like bands of one spin flavor and an order which fills both TBG-like bands in the other spin flavor, resulting in a Dirac crossing where the TBG-like and graphene-like bands connect at the Fermi level.

D.2
Connection to Experimental Phase Diagram

We can connect the band structures at
\nu=0
and
\nu=2
to the experimental phase diagram; at
\nu=0
, the leading instability is either a semimetallic IVC
-
state or an insulating sublattice polarized state. Either of these states can be related to the region of high resisitivity at
\nu=0
which persists both at
D_{0}=0
and
|D_{0}|>0
. At
\nu=2
, the spin polarized IVC
-
is still the leading phase at
D_{0}=0
and for a finite range of
D_{0}
. However, unlike at
\nu=0
, the IVC
-
is metallic at
\nu=2
. We note the lack of a strong high-resistivity state at
\nu=2
when
D_{0}=0
in the experimental phase diagram. For large enough
D_{0}
, the IVC
-
state transitions to a semimetallic spin-polarized SLP state. This transition may be related to the appearance of a state with higher resisitivity at
\nu=2
for a finite value of
D_{0}
in the experimental phase diagram.

Figure 12
:
We show the energies resulting from our HF calculations at
\nu=2
. As at
\nu=0
, the spin-polarized IVC
-
is dominant relative to a spin-valley polarized state, except for a region of smaller
w_{0}
and large
D_{0}
where a spin-polarized sublattice polarized state dominates. We note that the spin-valley polarized state is closer in energy to the spin-polarized IVC
-
state as we vary
D_{0}
in our
\nu=2
calculation than at
\nu=0
.

Figure 13
:
Band structures obtained at
\nu=2
with
\epsilon=7
,
d_{s}=40
nm for the full set of spin-polarized versions of ansätze in Table
3
.

Appendix E
Band Structure and solution for
D_{0}\sim w_{1}

In Sec.
IV
, we studied phases varying
D_{0}
up to half the value of
w_{1}
. We will here discuss the behavior of the phases we find varying
D_{0}
up to and past
w_{1}
. We find our numerics begin not to converge for
D_{0}/w_{1}>1
for some values of
w_{0}
we study. We
will focus here on the behavior of the valley polarized state at
\nu=0
and the spin polarized version of this state at
\nu=2
but note the similarities between the behavior of this state and the sublattice polarized states and IVC states where they converge in the HF numerics.

At
\nu=0
, as stated in the main text, the valley polarized solution becomes metallic as
D_{0}
increases and the bands in either minivalley are pushed away from the Fermi level. For
D_{0}/w_{1}
