relatively small, the bands in each valley flavor cross the Fermi level only near the K and K’ points. However as
D_{0}/w_{1}\rightarrow 1
, the bandwidth of the valley-polarized TBG-like bands increases, resulting in additional Fermi surfaces near the
\Gamma
point. For large
D_{0}
, the lower TBG-like band which begins above the Fermi level at small
D_{0}
is pushed completely below the Fermi level and, for large enough
D_{0}
, the band structure reverts to a filled lower bands solution.

At
\nu=2
, the band structure of the solutions we obtain via HF for the spin-valley polarized ansatz also begin with separated Fermi-surfaces around the K and K’ points and no Fermi surfaces near
\Gamma
. Similar to the
\nu=0
case, as
D_{0}
increases, the bandwidth of the polarized TBG-like bands increases, leading to changes in the Fermi surface, with additional hole-like Fermi surfaces appearing near the
\Gamma
point at intermediate values of
D_{0}
until the lower polarized band which begins above the Fermi level is pushed below the Fermi level for large
D_{0}
. Unlike at
\nu=0
, the solution at large
D_{0}
retains a finite valley polarization. Experimental Hall densities measured in MSTG show a change in sign of the Hall density at half filling for large
D_{0}
at
|\nu|=2
. In the main text we argued this effect could be accounted for by the merging of Fermi surfaces of the IVC
-
bands (or a similar transition of the SLP
-
phase) near the K and K’ point doping slightly away from
\nu=2
when
D_{0}>0
. Should the experimental value of the displacement field at which the sign change appears exceed the values of the displacement field discussed in the main text, it is possible that instead the sign change could be related to the dramatic changes in Fermi surfaces we observe and discuss here. We show how the band structure of the valley polarized state evolves at large
D_{0}
in Fig.
14
for
\nu=0
and in Fig.
15
for
|\nu|=2
. The IVC
-
and sublattice polarized states display similar behavior at charge-neutrality and
\nu=2
.

Another general feature we note is a tendency after a critical value of
D_{0}/w_{1}>1
for all of our orders to converge to solutions which are nearly degenerate in energy. While some retain the symmetry breaking in their initial ansatz, the solutions generally have a preference to fill the lower bands up to the chemical potential at
\nu=0
and
\nu=2
and exhibit additional Fermi surfaces at large
D_{0}
. Our numerics do not always converge in this region, so we do not include it in our phase diagrams or energies which are limited to
D_{0}/w_{1}<0.5
.

Figure 14
:
We show the band structure of the VP state at
\nu=0
as we vary
D_{0}
for
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.55
. At charge neutrality, the VP state at
D_{0}
comparable to
w_{1}
acquires additional hole-like Fermi surfaces. We hi-light a single valley-spin flavor band in yellow to show the evolution of a single flavor at large
D_{0}
.

Figure 15
:
We show the band structure of the spin-VP phase at
\nu=2
as we vary
D_{0}
for
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.33
. We note at half-filling both the spin-valley polarized and intervalley coherent states at
D_{0}
comparable to
w_{1}
acquire additional hole-like Fermi surfaces. We hi-light a single valley-spin flavor band in yellow to show where the additional Fermi surfaces appear as a function of
D_{0}
.

Appendix F
Different contributions to the energy

In this appendix, we detail how the different energetic contributions for particle-hole instabilities, discussed briefly in Sec.
V
of the main text, are derived and what their analytical form is. We use these expressions to compute their respective scaling with system parameters, summarized in Table
3
.

Our starting point are Slater-determinant states with correlation matrix
P^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=(\mathbbm{1}+Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}})/2
, see Eq. (
25
) for its definition, where
Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is block diagonal in the graphene-like-TBG-like space and of the form

Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\begin{pmatrix}Q^{\text{g},0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}&0\\
0&Q^{\text{b},0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\end{pmatrix},\qquad Q^{\text{g},0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-\sigma_{z},

(127)

i.e., further assume that the graphene-like bands do not develop any order while we take several different candidate orders,
Q^{\text{b},0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, in the TBG-like band to be discussed shortly.
Equation (
127
) is motivated by the following observations: first, at
D_{0}=0
, all order parameters can be classified as mirror-even (diagonal in graphene-TBG space) and mirror-odd (off-diagonal in graphene-TBG space). Based on the band structure, it is natural to expect that the former are energetically favored. In fact, we will see in Appendix
F.4
explicitly and in our HF numerics [see second row in Fig.
17
] as well as in our analysis of exact eigenstates in Appendix
B
that off-diagonal components are not favored at
D_{0}=0
.
Second,
Q^{\text{g},0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-\sigma_{z}
is motivated by the observations of Appendix
B
that the manifold of exact ground states of the MSTG Hamiltonian has this form for
D_{0}=0
in a finite range of coupling parameters [
\lambda
in Eq. (
18
)] between the graphene cones and the TBG bands. Again, this will be confirmed by our HF numerics, where the lowest-energy solutions are of the form of Eq. (
127
) in the flat-decoupled limit (
D_{0}=W_{\text{TBG}}=0
), and our discussion of order in the graphene-like bands in Appendix
F.3
below.

In Sec.
III.3
of the main text, we have constructed the different possible ansätze,
Q^{\text{b},0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, using the
(\text{U}(4)\times\text{U}(4))_{\text{b,cf}}
symmetry. We will complement this here by deriving the exact same set of states purely within HF.
As a first step, let us focus on minimizing the Fock energy in Eq. (
117d
) at
D_{0}=w_{0}=0
:

E_{\text{F}}[P^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=E_{\text{F,g}}-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\text{tr}\left[P^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(F^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}})^{T}P^{\text{b}}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q})}(F^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}})^{*}\right],\quad P^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbbm{1}+Q^{\text{b},0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),

(128)

where
E_{\text{F,g}}
is the Fock energy of the graphene subsystem being in a semimetallic state and
F^{bb}
the form factors in TBG subspace, which obey [cf. Eq. (
79a
)]

\bar{F}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})=\bar{F}^{bb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta_{0}\sigma_{0}s_{0}+i\bar{F}^{bb}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\eta_{0}\sigma_{2}s_{0}+\mathcal{O}(w_{0},D_{0}^{2}).

(129)

Since
\braket{A,B}=\text{tr}[AB]
defines an inner product on Hermitian matrices, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality holds,
\braket{A,B}\leq\sqrt{\braket{A,A}}\sqrt{\braket{B,B}}
with maximum reached when
A=cB
with
c>0
. Applying this to the second term in Eq. (
128
), we see that this term reaches its minimum when
Liu et al. 2021b

P^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=C_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}(F^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}})^{T}P^{\text{b}}_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q})}(F^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}})^{*},\quad C_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}>0,\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}.

(130)

In general, solving Eq. (
130
) can be difficult (or impossible) and the minimum of
E_{\text{F}}[P^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}]
requires a momentum-dependent correlator. However, for
D_{0}=w_{0}=0
, it holds
(F^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}})^{T}(F^{bb}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}})^{*}=\gamma_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\mathbbm{1}
with
\gamma_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}=\sum_{j=1,2}(\bar{F}^{bb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}>0
. Then, Eq. (
130
) is obeyed as long as

\left[Q^{\text{b},0}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\eta_{0}\sigma_{2}s_{0}\right]=0.

(131)

This is the same commutator relation as in Eq. (
29
). All momentum independent states that obey this property, as well as their symmetries are summarized in Table
2
.
Furthermore, we have also checked all of the states in Table
2
are also exactly degenerate in the Hartree term (
117c
) and in the additional subtraction point contribution in Eq. (
119
), as long as
w_{0}=D_{0}=0
.

In the following subsections, we will discuss the various energetic corrections to
Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
in Eq. (
127
) once we turn on
D_{0}
,
w_{0}
, and
W_{\text{TBG}}
, and allow the graphene-like bands to develop order and mix with the TBG-like bands in the vicinity of the Dirac cones. To simplify the discussion, we will first focus on the SU(2)
{}_{+}\times
SU(2)
-
-symmetric model defined in Sec.
II
. In that case, certain pairs of states, referred to Hund’s partners in
Scheurer and Samajdar 2020
, are guaranteed to be degenerate. Therefore, we will focus, without loss of generality, on only one member of each pair of Hund’s partners; for concreteness, we choose those above the vertical line in Table
2
. In Sec.
F.5
, we will study how these degeneracies are lifted for
J_{H}\neq 0
in Eq. (
30
).

Figure 16
:
HF energies relative to the SP state obtained by plugging in the ansätze in Table
2
in the HF energy expression. The behavior agrees with the analytical prediction in the column
\Delta E_{bb}(W_{\text{TBG}}=0)
in Table
3
.

F.1
Deformation of the form factors

Let us first analyze the energetic impact for these different phases due to the deformation of the form factors, see Eq. (
79
), as a consequence of
D_{0}
and
w_{0}
. To this end, we compute the change of the HF energy
E^{\text{HF}}
in Eq. (
117
) for
Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
in Eq. (
127
) for the different
Q^{\text{b}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
in Table
2
, setting
W_{\text{TBG}}=0
. Using the structure of the form factors in Eq. (
79
) along with the important constraints in Eqs. (
81
) and (
83
), we obtain after straightforward algebra the resultant change of energy
\Delta E_{bb}(W_{\text{TBG}}=0)
listed in Table
3
. The prefactors are given by

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left\{[\bar{F}_{3}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})]^{2}+[\bar{F}_{4}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})]^{2}\right\},

(132)

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{8}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}}V(\boldsymbol{G})\left[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bar{F}_{4}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})\right]^{2},

(133)

\displaystyle c_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left\{[\bar{F}_{5}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})]^{2}+[\bar{F}_{6}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})]^{2}\right\},

(134)

\displaystyle c_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left\{[\bar{F}_{7}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})]^{2}+[\bar{F}_{8}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})]^{2}\right\}.

(135)

Due to Eq. (
84
), we expect the Hartree contribution,
c_{2}
, to be numerically smaller than
c_{1}
. We have verified the form of these energy contributions wihtin our HF numerics, see Fig.
16
.

F.2
Finite bandwidth of the TBG-like bands

Next, we allow for
W_{\text{TBG}}\neq 0
in Eq. (
58
) by treating it as a perturbation to the different states in Table
2
.

F.2.1
First order perturbation theory

The first-order contribution is readily found by evaluating the expectation value of
\Delta H_{0}:=H_{0}-H_{0}|_{W_{\text{TBG}}=0}
with
H_{0}
given in Eq. (
58
) with respect to the states
\ket{\psi[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]}
. Within the matrix notation introduced in Appendix
C
, the correction to the energy can also be written as

\Delta E^{(1)}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\text{tr}\left[P^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})-h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})|_{W_{\text{TBG}}=0})\right].

(136)

Using the form of the kinetic energy of the TBG-like bands given in Eq. (
66
) and that
\bar{E}_{1}^{b}(\boldsymbol{k})=-\bar{E}_{1}^{b}(-\boldsymbol{k})
, one finds

\Delta E^{(1)}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\bar{E}_{3}^{b}(\boldsymbol{k})\,w_{0}D_{0}

(137)

for all states of Table
2
. Consequently, we have to go to second order in
W_{\text{TBG}}
to find a contribution that favors one of the orders over the others. This is what we will discuss next.

F.2.2
Second order perturbation theory: superexchange

For the discussion of second order perturbation theory, it will most convenient to switch back to the second quantization description. The unperturbed Hamiltonian is the full Hamiltonian in the flat-band limit,
H_{\text{I}}=H_{0}|_{W_{\text{TBG}}=0}+\widetilde{H}_{1}
, with
\widetilde{H}_{1}
given in Eq. (
77
), and the perturbation is the dispersion in the TBG-like bands, i.e.,

H_{\text{II}}=W_{\text{TBG}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\sum_{\eta=\pm}\sum_{s=\uparrow,\downarrow}\sum_{p=\pm}\epsilon_{(b,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})b^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};p,\eta,s}=W_{\text{TBG}}\sum_{j=0}^{3}H_{\text{II}}^{(j)},\quad H_{\text{II}}^{(j)}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}E^{b}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k})\,b^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}M_{j}b^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}.

(138)

Here we used the parameterization in Eq. (
66
) and introduced the matrices

M_{0}=\sigma_{3}\eta_{0}s_{0},\qquad M_{1}=\sigma_{0}\eta_{3}s_{0},\qquad M_{2}=\sigma_{3}\eta_{3}s_{0},\qquad M_{3}=\sigma_{0}\eta_{0}s_{0}

(139)

acting in the TBG-like subspace. As will become useful shortly, this decomposition of the kinetic energy has the property that all
M_{j}
defined in Eq. (
139
) either commute or anticommute with all of the
Q^{b}
in Table
2
separately.

Let us assume that (one of) the groundstate(s) of
H_{\text{I}}
with energy
E^{0}_{Q^{b}}
is the product state
\ket{\psi[Q^{b}]}
, characterized by the correlation matrix
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}=(\mathbbm{1}+\text{diag}(-\sigma_{z},Q^{b}))/2
in Eq. (
111
). Here,
Q^{b}
can be any of the states listed in Table
2
.
The energy correction to second order in
W_{\text{TBG}}
has the form

\Delta E^{(2)}=-\sum_{\ket{n}\neq\ket{\psi[Q^{b}]}}\frac{|\braket{n|H_{\text{II}}|\psi[Q^{b}]}|^{2}}{E^{0}_{n}-E^{0}_{Q^{b}}},

(140)

where the sum involves all eigenstates
\ket{n}
of
H_{\text{I}}
(excluding
\ket{\psi[Q^{b}]}
) with energy
E^{0}_{n}
. Assuming that the system is in an insulating phase in the TBG-like sector, we will have a finite gap,
E^{0}_{n}>E^{0}_{Q^{b}}
, such that perturbation theory is controlled in bandwidth over gap and
\Delta E^{(2)}\leq 0
, i.e., the “superexchange”-like processes discussed here, if finite, will lower the energy.

We will next show that for a given
Q^{b}
, all terms
j
in Eq. (
138
) with
[M_{j},Q^{b}]=0
will not contribute to Eq. (
140
) and, hence, only those that obey
\{M_{j},Q^{b}\}=0
can lower the energy to order
W_{\text{TBG}}^{2}
. To see this, first note that
[M_{j_{0}},Q^{b}]=0
implies that there exists a unitary matrix
V
such that both
V^{\dagger}Q^{b}V
and
V^{\dagger}M_{j_{0}}V
are diagonal. Since
\text{tr}\,Q^{b}=0
, we can take
V^{\dagger}Q^{b}V=\text{diag}(1,1,1,1,-1,-1,-1,-1)
without loss of generality at
\nu=0
. Consequently, the associated many-body state assumes the form

\ket{\psi[Q^{b}]}=\prod_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}\prod_{v=1}^{4}\bar{b}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};v}\ket{\psi_{0,g}},\qquad\bar{b}_{\boldsymbol{k};v}:=\sum_{\alpha}b_{\boldsymbol{k};\alpha}V_{\alpha,v},

(141)

where
\ket{\psi_{0,g}}
is the state of empty TBG-like bands and lower-filled graphene-like bands. Upon noting that
M^{T}_{j}=M_{j}
and
M_{j}^{2}=M_{j}
, we also see

H_{\text{II}}^{(j_{0})}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}E^{b}_{j_{0}}(\boldsymbol{k})\,\sum_{v=1}^{8}r_{v}\bar{b}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k};v}\bar{b}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k};v},\qquad r_{v}=\pm 1.

(142)

Consequently, we get

\braket{n|H_{\text{II}}^{(j_{0})}|\psi[Q^{b}]}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\text{MBZ}}E^{b}_{j_{0}}(\boldsymbol{k})\,\sum_{v=1}^{4}r_{v}\braket{n|\psi[Q^{b}]}=0

(143)

since
\ket{\psi[Q^{b}]}
and
\ket{n}
are orthogonal for all terms in the sum of Eq. (
140
).

From this algebraic criterion, we can immediately read off that the SP and VP states have
\Delta E^{(2)}=0
, i.e., cannot gain energy from this “superexchange” mechanism. The SLP
±
and SSLP
±
order parameters, however, anticommute with
M_{0}
and
M_{2}
. As such, they can benefit both from the superexchange processes associated with
E_{0}^{b}=\bar{E}_{0}^{b}
(already present when
w_{0}=D_{0}=0
, but
\theta
tuned away from the magic angle) and
E_{2}^{b}=\bar{E}_{2}^{b}D_{0}
(unique to the trilayer system and only non-zero for
D_{0}\neq 0
). Furthermore, while both IVCs can benefit from
E_{1}^{b}=\bar{E}_{1}^{b}w_{0}
, the IVC
-
(IVC
+
) also benefits from
E_{0}^{b}
(
E_{2}^{b}=\bar{E}_{2}^{b}D_{0}
). This leads to the results shown in Table
3
.

F.3
Ordering in the graphene-like bands

Let us next also take into account that the coupling between the graphene-like and TBG-like bands can modify the ansatz in Eq. (
127
). Note that a perturbative treatment of bandwidth of the graphene-like bands, similar to our analysis in Sec.
F.2
, is not controlled as these bands are highly dispersive and their bandwidth and energy,
\epsilon_{(g,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})
, at generic momentum points are larger than the interaction energy scale. At the same time,
\epsilon_{(g,p),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})
is small around the K/K’ points such that taking the interaction as a small parameter—as employed in the full quantum mechanical analysis in Appendix
B
—is not necessarily valid either for realistic system parameters. Therefore, we here use a different approach that takes advantage of these observations: while the diagonal structure of Eq. (
127
) and
Q^{g}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-\sigma_{z}
are likely to be a good approximation far from the K/K’ points, it is expected to be modified in the vicinity of these points.

To formalize this, we define
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{+}\cup\mathcal{A}_{-}
where
\mathcal{A}_{+}
(
\mathcal{A}_{-}
) is a region of momentum points around the K (K’) point. The precise form does not matter, we only need that its area,
V_{\mathcal{A}}
, is small compared to the overall size of the MBZ. We now take

Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\begin{cases}Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\quad\boldsymbol{k}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}},\\
Q^{1}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\quad\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A},\end{cases}\qquad\bar{\mathcal{A}}:=\text{MBZ}\setminus\mathcal{A}

(144)

with
Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
given in Eq. (
127
), and accordingly for
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}
. Expanding the change of the HF energy,
\Delta E^{\text{HF}}=E^{\text{HF}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}]-E^{\text{HF}}[P_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{0}]
, to leading order in
V_{\mathcal{A}}
, one finds after straightforward algebra

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta E^{\text{HF}}&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\text{tr}[\delta Q^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})]+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}V(\boldsymbol{G})\,\text{Re}\left\{\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}}\text{tr}[P^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}F^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G}}]\right)\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\text{tr}[\delta Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}F^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}]\right\}\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}}V(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{G})\text{tr}[\delta Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}F^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{G}}P^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}F^{*}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{G}}]\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}V(\boldsymbol{G})\Gamma^{0}_{\boldsymbol{G}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\text{tr}[\delta Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}^{T}]+\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\text{tr}[\delta Q^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}}F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}]+\mathcal{O}(V^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}),\end{split}

(145)

where we defined the shorthand
\delta Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}=Q^{1}_{\boldsymbol{k}}-Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
\Gamma^{0}_{\boldsymbol{G}}=8\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\left(F^{bb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})+F^{gg}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})\right)
. Here, the first, second, and third terms come from the dispersion, Hartree, and Fock term, while the last line is due to the subtraction point.
To simplify further, note that we can extend the sums over
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
in Eq. (
145
) to the entire MBZ without changing the result at linear order in
V_{\mathcal{A}}
. Focusing on the relevant case of
P_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{0}=P^{0}
, we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta E^{\text{HF}}&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\text{tr}[\delta Q^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})]\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}\in\text{RL}}V(\boldsymbol{G})\,\text{Re}\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\text{tr}[Q^{0}F^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G}}]\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\text{tr}[\delta Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}F^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G}}]\right)\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left(\text{tr}[\delta Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}F^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}P^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}F^{*}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}]-\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}[\delta Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{T}F_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{*}]\right)+\mathcal{O}(V^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}).\end{split}

(146)

Postponing the discussion of
Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}
with non-zero off-diagonal components that mix the graphene-like and TBG-like bands to the next subsection, we here focus on block diagonal

Q^{1}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\begin{pmatrix}Q^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}&0\\
0&Q^{b}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\end{pmatrix},

(147)

where
Q^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
will be chosen to describe the same order as in the TBG-like bands, i.e., to break/keep exactly the same symmetries as the considered order parameter in the TBG-like bands.

Let us begin our discussion with the
spin-polarized
state, i.e., take

\left(Q^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\begin{cases}\sigma_{0}s_{3},\qquad\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}\\
-\sigma_{3}s_{0},\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(148)

in Eq. (
144
).
Using Eq. (
146
), it is a matter of straightforward algebra to show that the associated change of the energy is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta E^{\text{HF}}_{\text{SpinPol}}&\sim 2\sum_{\eta}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}\bar{E}^{g}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k};\eta)\\
&-\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sum_{\eta}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[(\bar{F}_{2,\eta}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}_{1,\eta}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\right]\\
&-\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sum_{\eta}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[(\bar{F}_{1,\eta}^{bg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}_{2,\eta}^{bg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\right]D_{0}^{2}\\
&-\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sum_{\eta}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[(\bar{F}_{7,\eta}^{bg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}_{8,\eta}^{bg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\right]w_{0}^{2}D_{0}^{2}\\
&-\frac{2}{N}\Bigl\{\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sum_{\eta}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[(\bar{F}_{7,\eta}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}_{8,\eta}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\right]+2\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}}V(\boldsymbol{G})\sum_{\eta^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bar{F}^{gg}_{8,\eta^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G})\sum_{\eta,\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}\bar{F}^{gg}_{8,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})\Bigr\}w_{0}^{2}D_{0}^{4}\end{split}

(149)

where
\bar{E}^{g}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k};\eta)
is (the part of) the dispersion of the graphene-like bands defined in Eq. (
67
). To keep the notation more compact, we further defined the
\eta
-dependent form-factor components as

\displaystyle F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})

\displaystyle=F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})+\eta\,F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j+2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}),\qquad j=1,2,

(150)

\displaystyle F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})

\displaystyle=F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})+\eta\,F^{tt^{\prime}}_{j-2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}),\qquad j=7,8.

(151)

To understand the scaling behavior of the first contribution associated with the kinetic term, let us take
\bar{E}^{g}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k};\eta)=\alpha|D_{0}|+v_{D}|\delta_{\eta}\boldsymbol{k}|
where
\delta_{\eta}\boldsymbol{k}
is the distance from the K (K’) point in valley
\eta=+
(
\eta=-
) and
\alpha>0
. As such we expect the scaling behavior

\sum_{\eta}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}\bar{E}^{g}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k};\eta)\sim c_{1}|D_{0}|(\Delta k)^{2}+c_{2}v_{D}(\Delta k)^{3},\qquad c_{j}>0,

(152)

as a function of
D_{0}
and the linear size,
\Delta k
, of
\mathcal{A}
. While we have already indicated the scaling behavior of all remaining terms in Eq. (
149
) with
D_{0}
and
w_{0}
, we further note that they scale with the area of
\mathcal{A}
and, thus, quadratically with
\Delta k
.

To discuss the consequences, let us first focus on
D_{0}=0
. We see that the energetic penalty due to the kinetic term scales as
\Delta k^{3}
while the energetic gain from the second line of Eq. (
149
) scales as
\Delta k^{2}
. One could naively conclude that this implies that the system should lower its energy by polarizing the graphene bands in some vicinity of its Dirac cones. Closer inspection, however, shows the involved energetics is equivalent to that of single-layer graphene: note that, for
D_{0}=0
, the first two lines of Eq. (
149
) involve the dispersion and interaction matrix elements only in the graphene subspace. Since we know that graphene is not a spin-polarized insulator, this spin polarization cannot be preferred by the system. In fact, one can show that the two terms in the second line of Eq. (
149
) cancel each other to leading order in
\Delta k
: To see this, let us focus on the contribution of one valley, say
\eta=+
, and consider
\boldsymbol{k}\rightarrow-\boldsymbol{q}_{1}/2
(more precisely
\boldsymbol{k}=k(\cos\phi_{0},\sin\phi_{0})-\boldsymbol{q}_{1}/2
and
k\rightarrow 0^{+}
) where we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[(\bar{F}_{2,+}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}_{1,+}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\xi_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{q})}-\phi_{0}}{2}\right)-\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\xi_{\text{MBZ}(\boldsymbol{q})}-\phi_{0}}{2}\right)\right]\delta_{\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{q}},0},

(153)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}\in\text{MBZ}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\xi_{\boldsymbol{q}}-\phi_{0}}{2}\right)-\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\xi_{\boldsymbol{q}}-\phi_{0}}{2}\right)\right].

(154)

Here we inserted the explicit form of the form factors given in Eq. (
85
). This expression has to vanish since
V(\boldsymbol{q})=V(-\boldsymbol{q})
,
\xi_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\xi_{-\boldsymbol{q}}+\pi
, and
\boldsymbol{q}\in\text{MBZ}
implies
-\boldsymbol{q}\in\text{MBZ}
. The same analysis can be performed for the other valley
\eta=-
. For this reason, also the interaction correction in Eq. (
149
) scales (at least) as
(\Delta k)^{3}
for
D_{0}=0
and, hence, does not generically dominate the kinetic contribution of the first line.

Turning on
D_{0}
, we see that while there can be some additional gain starting at order
D_{0}^{2}
, this gain is always overcompensated for sufficiently small
D_{0}
by the additional cost from the kinetic energy which scales as
D_{0}
.

These results are completely consistent with our HF numerics: as can be seen in Fig.
3
, at
D_{0}=0
, the graphene Dirac cones are not split. Furthermore, while there are spin-split Dirac cones around the Fermi level at K/K’ for
D_{0}\neq 0
, these are not related to the spin-ordering in the graphene-like bands defined in Eq. (
148
). This becomes obvious by noting that both spin flavors of the upper (lower) graphene-like band are unoccopied (occupied), as is clearly visible based on the band connectivity. Furthermore, we can see it in the correlator of the SP around the K/K’ points shown in Fig.
17
.

All other states in Table
2
can be analyzed in a similar way. To begin with the
valley polarized
state, the analogue of Eq. (
148
) reads as

Q^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\begin{cases}\sigma_{0}\eta_{3}s_{0},\qquad\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A},\\
-\sigma_{3}\eta_{0}s_{0},\quad\,\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(155)

which leads to a kinetic energy contribution [from the first line of Eq. (
146
)]

\Delta E^{\text{kin}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\text{tr}[\delta Q^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{k}(\boldsymbol{k})]=2\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\eta}\left(\bar{E}_{0}^{g}(\boldsymbol{k},\eta)+\eta\bar{E}_{1}^{g}(\boldsymbol{k},\eta)w_{0}D_{0}\right)=2\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\eta}\bar{E}_{0}^{g}(\boldsymbol{k},\eta),

(156)

where (although not crucial for the following conclusion), we made the natural assumption
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}\Leftrightarrow-\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}
in the last equality. Most importantly, we see that the kinetic energy contribution now scales as
(\Lambda+D_{0})(\Delta k)^{2}
to leading order in
\Delta k
, where
\Lambda
is the large energy of the graphene-like remote band, i.e., in the valley that does not have a Dirac cone at the respective K point
\boldsymbol{k}
is close to. As such, it is energetically not favorable to have valley polarization of the graphene-like bands around the K/K’ points.

As is intuitively clear and can be derived in the same way, this suppression due to the kinetic energy also applies to the
IVC states
in Table
2
. One also finds exactly the same expression,
\Delta E^{\text{kin}}=2\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\eta}\bar{E}_{0}^{g}(\boldsymbol{k},\eta)\sim(\Lambda+D_{0})(\Delta k)^{2}
. Consequently, ordering of the graphene-like bands in the vicinity of the K/K’ points can also be excluded for the IVC states.

For the
sublattice polarized
states, SLP
±
, the situation is slightly more complicated since the structure in the graphene-like bands is in general of the form

\displaystyle\left(Q^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\begin{cases}(-\sigma_{3}\cos\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\sigma_{2}\sin\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}})s_{0},\qquad&\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}\\
-\sigma_{3}s_{0},\quad&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(157)

and it is left to determine the optimal
\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}\in\mathbb{R}
subject to the constraint
\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\mp\theta_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
for the SLP
±
state resulting from
C_{2z}
and
\Theta
. Note that
(Q^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{k}})^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
and
\text{tr}\,Q^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=0
, hold for any
\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and, hence, does not further constrain it.

Making the natural assumption that
\sin\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is significantly non-zero only in the small region
\mathcal{A}
around the K/K
′
points, we can still use Eq. (
146
) which yields

\Delta E^{\text{HF}}_{\text{SLP}_{\pm}}[\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=\sum_{\eta}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}\left[A_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}(1-\cos\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}})+B_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\sin\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right],

(158)

where

\displaystyle A_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}

\displaystyle=2\bar{E}^{g}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k},\eta)+\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[(\bar{F}_{1,\eta}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}_{2,\eta}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}(D_{0}^{4}),

(159a)

\displaystyle B_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[(\bar{F}_{1,\eta}^{gb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}_{2,\eta}^{gb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\right]D_{0}^{2}+\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[(\bar{F}_{7,\eta}^{gb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}_{8,\eta}^{gb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\right]w_{0}^{2}D_{0}^{2}.

(159b)

First, note that
B_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=0
for
D_{0}=0
. Furthermore, the second term in Eq. (
159a
) is suppressed to leading order in
\Delta k
, as already discussed above, such that
A_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}>0
and, thus,
\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}=0
for
D_{0}=0
. This agrees with our result of Appendix
B
and with the HF band structure in Fig.
3
which exhibits a gapless graphene Dirac cone at
D_{0}=0
for the SLP
-
(the same applies for SLP
+
, not shown). More explicitly, this can also be seen in the upper panel of Fig.
17
, where the correlators of the SLP
±
states close to the K point at
D_{0}=0
are shown: the graphene bands are just in the lower-filled-band configuration.

The system behaves differently when
D_{0}\neq 0
. We then have
B_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\neq 0
and it will become energetically favorable to develop finite SLP
±
order in the graphene-like bands according to
\tan\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\widetilde{B}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\pm}/\widetilde{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
,
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
, where we defined

\widetilde{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=A_{\boldsymbol{k},+}+A_{-\boldsymbol{k},-},\qquad\widetilde{B}^{\pm}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=B_{\boldsymbol{k},+}\mp B_{-\boldsymbol{k},-},

(160)

with
+
(
-
) for the SLP
+
(SLP
-
) case. The associated minimized energy change is given by

\min_{\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\Delta E^{\text{HF}}_{\text{SLP}_{\pm}}[\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}}\left[\widetilde{A}_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\sqrt{\widetilde{A}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+(\widetilde{B}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\pm})^{2}}\right].

(161)

Taken together, both SLP
±
states will develop order in the graphene-like bands around the K and K’ points as long as
D_{0}
is non-zero. As
\widetilde{A}\sim D_{0}
and
\widetilde{B}^{\pm}\sim D^{2}_{0}
, the associated energy gain will scale as
-g_{\pm}V_{\mathcal{A}}|D_{0}|^{3}
for small
D_{0}
. As follows from Eq. (
159b
),
B_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}
has a definite sign for sufficiently small
w_{0}
, such that
|\widetilde{B}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{-}|>|\widetilde{B}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{+}|
and, thus,
g_{-}>g_{+}
, i.e., the SLP
-
state can gain more energy than the SLP
+
phase from this process for small
w_{0}
. Since
\sin\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}\neq 0
in Eq. (
157
) corresponds to a mixing of the upper and lower bands of the graphene-like bands, this will gap out the Dirac cones of the SLP
±
states at
D_{0}\neq 0
and we obtain an insulator rather than a semimetal; this is also seen in our HF numerics (cf. Fig.
3
). Furthermore, it is directly visible in the correlators of the SLP
±
states shown in the lower panel of Fig.
17
; the additional mixing of the TBG-like and graphene-like band that can also be seen in the HF data will be analyzed in Appendix
F.4
.

In analogy to these two states, we choose for the
quantum spin Hall state
(SSLP
-
)

\displaystyle\left(Q^{\text{g}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\begin{cases}-\sigma_{3}s_{0}\cos\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\sigma_{2}s_{3}\sin\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}},\qquad&\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}\\
-\sigma_{3}s_{0},\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(162)

The corresponding change of the energy has again the form of Eq. (
158
), this time with

A_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=2\bar{E}^{g}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k},\eta)+\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[(\bar{F}_{1,\eta}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}_{2,\eta}^{gg}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}(w_{0}^{2}D_{0}^{4}),\qquad B_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=0.

(163)

Note that the vanishing of
B_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}
is consistent with the fact that the energy should not depend on whether
\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\theta_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
(SSLP
-
) or
\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-\theta_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
(SSLP
+
), since these two states are related by a SU(2)
{}_{+}\times
SU(2)
-
transformation. We clearly see that the energy is minimized by
\cos{\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}}=1
, i.e., no order in the graphene-like bands and no associated energetic gain. We emphasize that the presence of SSLP
-
order in the TBG-like bands and, at
D_{0}\neq 0
, interactions that couple the two subsystems, also induces a gap in the original graphene Dirac cone; we will see this in Appendix
F.4
below where we will find a non-zero mixing of the TBG-like and graphene-like bands for the SSLP states.

F.4
Mixing between the TBG-like and graphene-like bands

Figure 17
:
We show the projector
P
which characterizes each of our symmetry breaking solution for a point near the
\Gamma
point of the Brillouin zone and
K
point of the Brillouin zone for
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.73
and
D_{0}/w_{1}=0
(top) and
D_{0}/w_{1}=0.5
(bottom). We plot the matrix form of
P
such that the largest block shown outlined in blue in the first panel of the top row denotes spin flavor, the next smallest blocks outlined in red denote valley flavor, and the next smallest block outlined in green denotes upper and lower bands in the continuum model and final two boxes denoting TBG-like or graphene-like band index in the continuum model, where the final two boxes denote the graphene-like and TBG-like band in the upper-band box and the TBG-like then graphene-like bands in the lower-band box.

We have not yet taken into account the possibility that the TBG-like and graphene-like bands can mix or, in other words, develop some coherence in the vicinity of the K/K
′
points; this corresponds to allowing for off-diagonal components in
Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}
. It is clear by symmetry that such a mixing is only possible for
D_{0}\neq 0
.

To describe the mixing between the bands, we write

Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}=U_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}U_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger},\qquad Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\begin{pmatrix}-\sigma_{z}\eta_{0}s_{0}&0\\
0&Q^{b}\end{pmatrix}\qquad U_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}U_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}=\mathbbm{1},

(164)

where
Q^{b}
is any of the order parameters in the TBG-like bands listed in Table
2
and the unitary matrix
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, which mixes the TBG-like and graphene-like bands, is constrained by the symmetries of the state
Q^{b}
. Note that unitarity of
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
guarantees
Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
,
Q^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=Q^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, and
\text{tr}\,Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}=0
.

Since are interested in mixing of the bands, we will focus on
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
of the form

U_{\boldsymbol{k}}=e^{i\varphi\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}}=e^{i\varphi\sum_{j=1,2}\sum_{j^{\prime}}\hat{n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{(j,j^{\prime})}M^{(j,j^{\prime})}\zeta_{j}},\qquad\left(M^{(j,j^{\prime})}\right)^{\dagger}=M^{(j,j^{\prime})},\quad\hat{n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{(j,j^{\prime})}\in\mathbbm{R}

(165)

where
\zeta_{j}
are Pauli matrices acting between the TBG-like and graphene-like bands and
M^{(j,j^{\prime})}
are matrices in spin, band, and valley space. It, thus, holds
[M^{(j_{1},j_{2})},\zeta_{j}]=0
. Before discussing the different candidate states separately, where the different symmetries will constrain the available generators in Eq. (
165
), let us expand Eq. (
164
) up to second order in the rotational angle
\varphi
. To this end, define
\mathcal{M}_{j,\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{j^{\prime}}\hat{n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{(j,j^{\prime})}M^{(j,j^{\prime})}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\mathcal{M}_{1,\boldsymbol{k}}+i\mathcal{M}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}
. Equation (
164
) then becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}&\sim\begin{pmatrix}-\sigma_{z}&0\\
0&Q^{\text{b}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&i\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}Q^{\text{b}}+i\sigma_{z}\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\\
-iQ^{\text{b}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}-i\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}&0\end{pmatrix}\varphi\\
&\quad+\left[\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}Q^{\text{b}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}&0\\
0&-\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\{\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\sigma_{z}\}&0\\
0&\{\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}},Q^{\text{b}}\}\end{pmatrix}\right]\varphi^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi^{3}).\end{split}

(166)

Below, we will use this expression to interpret the mixing matrices we obtain analytically and to constrain the possible generators
M^{(j,j^{\prime})}
.

Since the IVC
-
is favored energetically in most of parameter space, let us start with the
intervalley coherent phases
, which can be discussed simultaneously. Due to the valley U(1)
v
symmetry, we can choose their “undeformed” correlator in the TBG-like subspace, without loss of generality, to be
Q^{b}=\eta_{x}
and
Q^{b}=\sigma_{y}\eta_{y}
for the IVC
+
and IVC
-
, respectively. As such, the phases still preserve the
C_{2z}\Theta
symmetry in Table
5
(for other choices, it has to be combined with a valley U(1)
v
rotation). Consequently,
M^{(j,j^{\prime})}
in Eq. (
165
) must obey

(M^{(1,j^{\prime})})^{*}=-M^{(1,j^{\prime})},\quad(M^{(2,j^{\prime})})^{*}=M^{(2,j^{\prime})},\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{M}^{*}=-\mathcal{M}.

(167)

Furthermore, the preserved spin-rotation symmetry forces
M_{j}
to be trivial in spin-space. This leaves the following
3+3=6
options for
M_{1}
and
3\times 3+1=10
possibilities for
M_{2}
,

M^{(1,j^{\prime})}\in\{\sigma_{y}\eta_{0,x,z},\sigma_{0,x,z}\eta_{y}\},\qquad M^{(2,j^{\prime})}\in\{\sigma_{0,x,z}\eta_{0,x,z},\sigma_{y}\eta_{y}\}.

(168)

However, not all (linear combinations) of the
16
generators are important for the IVC states. First, we can neglect all parts of
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}
in Eq. (
165
) that commute with
Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
. This can be done formally by setting
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}-Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}})/2
or, equivalently, by replacing

\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+Q^{\text{b}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}).

(169)

For future reference, this means that it holds

\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}=Q^{\text{b}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}.

(170)

This reduces the number of independent generators to
8
. To begin with the IVC
+
, a complete set of associated generators is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{y}\left(\sigma_{0}\eta_{0}+\sigma_{z}\eta_{x}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{y}\left(\sigma_{z}\eta_{0}+\sigma_{0}\eta_{x}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{y}\left(\sigma_{y}\eta_{y}-\sigma_{x}\eta_{z}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{x}\left(\sigma_{y}\eta_{z}+\sigma_{x}\eta_{y}\right),\qquad\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{y}\sigma_{x}\eta_{0}+\zeta_{x}\sigma_{y}\eta_{x}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{y}\sigma_{0}\eta_{z}+\zeta_{x}\sigma_{z}\eta_{y}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{y}\sigma_{x}\eta_{x}+\zeta_{x}\sigma_{y}\eta_{0}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}\eta_{z}+\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}\eta_{y}\right).\end{split}

(171)

For the IVC
-
, they read as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{y}\left(\sigma_{z}\eta_{0}+\sigma_{y}\eta_{y}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{y}\left(\sigma_{0}\eta_{x}+\sigma_{x}\eta_{z}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{y}\left(\sigma_{x}\eta_{x}-\sigma_{0}\eta_{z}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{x}\left(\sigma_{y}\eta_{0}+\sigma_{z}\eta_{y}\right),\qquad\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{x}\sigma_{y}\eta_{x}-\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}\eta_{z}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{x}\sigma_{y}\eta_{z}+\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}\eta_{x}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}\eta_{y}+\zeta_{y}\sigma_{x}\eta_{0}\right),\quad\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{x}\sigma_{x}\eta_{y}-\zeta_{y}\sigma_{0}\eta_{0}\right).\end{split}

(172)

To further constrain the remaining
8
generators, we note that the graphene-like bands in valley
\eta=-
(valley
\eta=+
) are at energies far away from the Fermi surface (of scale
\Lambda
) for
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
(
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{-}
). In the following, we will neglect all mixing processes that are suppressed in the limit of large
\Lambda
. Inspection of the first term in Eq. (
146
) shows that this is equivalent to demanding that
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
obey

\Delta_{\Lambda}(\eta)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}\text{tr}_{\text{g}}[(U_{\boldsymbol{k}}Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}U_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}-Q^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}})\hat{\Lambda}_{\eta}]=0,\qquad\hat{\Lambda}_{\pm}=\Lambda\sigma_{z}(\eta_{0}\mp\eta_{3})/2,

(173)

where the trace is only over the graphene-like subspace. Using Eq. (
166
), we can express this quantity as

\Delta_{\Lambda}(\eta)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}\left(\text{tr}_{\text{g}}[\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}Q^{\text{b}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\Lambda}_{\eta}]+\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}_{\text{g}}[\{\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\sigma_{z}\}\hat{\Lambda}_{\eta}]\right)\varphi^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi^{3}).

(174)

To simplify further, we use Eq. (
170
) and obtain

\Delta_{\Lambda}(\eta)=2\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}\text{tr}_{\text{g},-\eta}[\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}]\varphi^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varphi^{3}),

(175)

where the trace is only over the graphene-like subspace in valley
-\eta
. As such,
\Delta_{\Lambda}(\eta)=0
requires

\text{tr}_{\text{g},\mp}[\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=\sum_{s,s^{\prime},p,p^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}\left|(\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{(p^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}),(p,\mp,s)}\right|^{2}=0\quad\forall\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad(\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{(p^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}),(p,\mp,s)}=0\quad\forall\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}.

(176)

It is straightforward to see that there are
4
linear combinations of the
8
generators in Eq. (
171
) (in Eq. (
172
)) for the IVC
+
(IVC
-
) that satisfy this requirement. For
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
, these are

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left[\zeta_{y}(\sigma_{0}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})+\sigma_{z}\eta_{x})\pm\zeta_{x}\sigma_{z}\eta_{y}\right],\qquad\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left[\zeta_{x}(\sigma_{y}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})\pm\sigma_{x}\eta_{y})+\zeta_{y}\sigma_{x}\eta_{x}\right],

(177a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left[\zeta_{y}(\sigma_{z}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})+\sigma_{0}\eta_{x})\pm\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}\eta_{y}\right],\qquad\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left[\zeta_{y}(\sigma_{x}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})\mp\sigma_{y}\eta_{y})+\zeta_{x}\sigma_{y}\eta_{x}\right],

(177b)

for the IVC
+
and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left[\zeta_{x}(\sigma_{y}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})+\sigma_{z}\eta_{y})\pm\zeta_{y}\sigma_{z}\eta_{x}\right],\qquad\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left[\zeta_{y}(\sigma_{0}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})\mp\sigma_{x}\eta_{x})-\zeta_{x}\sigma_{x}\eta_{y}\right],

(178a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left[\zeta_{y}(\sigma_{z}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})+\sigma_{y}\eta_{y})\mp\zeta_{x}\sigma_{y}\eta_{x}\right],\qquad\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left[\zeta_{y}(\sigma_{x}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})\mp\sigma_{0}\eta_{x})+\zeta_{x}\sigma_{0}\eta_{y}\right],

(178b)

for the IVC
-
.

Taken together, we use
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}=e^{i\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}\lambda_{\pm}}
for
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
and
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\mathbbm{1}
otherwise, where
\lambda_{\pm}
is any of the four generators in Eq. (
177
) for the IVC
+
and any of those in Eq. (
178
) for the IVC
-
. From Eq. (
146
), we can compute the associated change of energy that is found to be of the form

\Delta E^{\text{HF}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}}\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sin^{2}\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sin\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cos\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}};

(179)

to reduce the summation to momenta in
\mathcal{A}_{+}
only, we have used
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\varphi_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
. For the IVC
+
with
Q^{b}=\eta_{x}
, this follows from the fact that
C_{2z}
as given in Table
5
acts as
\eta_{x}
and that
\eta_{x}\lambda_{\pm}\eta_{x}=\lambda_{\mp}
for all generators in Eq. (
177
). For the IVC
-
with
Q^{b}=\sigma_{y}\eta_{y}
,
C_{2z}
has to be combined with the U(1)
v
valley rotation
i\eta_{z}
to be a symmetry (with action on the spinors given by
\eta_{y}\sigma_{0}
). However, as follows from Eq. (
178
), it holds
\eta_{y}\lambda_{\pm}\eta_{y}=\lambda_{\mp}
in this case, leading to the same result,
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\varphi_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
.

For ease of presentation, let us discuss the behavior of
\Delta E^{\text{HF}}
separately for the two IVCs and begin with the IVC
+
state. Focusing on the limit
w_{0}=0
for now, we obtain
\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}=0
(and
\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}>0
at least in a finite range of
D_{0}
around
D_{0}=0
) for the generators in Eq. (
177b
) such that
\Delta E^{\text{HF}}
reaches its minimum
\Delta E^{\text{HF}}=0
when
\sin\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}=0
(no mixing). For the first and the second generator in Eq. (
177a
), we find

\displaystyle\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}

\displaystyle=4\left(2\epsilon_{(g,+),+}(\boldsymbol{k})-\sum_{\eta=\pm}\epsilon_{(b,+),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})\right)+\frac{8}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\bar{F}^{bb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right)^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{4}(-1)^{j}\left(\bar{F}^{gg}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right)^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}(D_{0}^{2}),

(180a)

\displaystyle\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[\sum_{j=1}^{4}\bar{F}^{gg}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})-\sum_{j=1}^{2}\bar{F}^{bb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{bg}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right]D_{0}+\mathcal{O}(D_{0}^{3}),

(180b)

and

\displaystyle\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}

\displaystyle=4\left(2\epsilon_{(g,+),+}(\boldsymbol{k})+\sum_{\eta=\pm}\epsilon_{(b,+),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})\right)+\frac{8}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\bar{F}^{bb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right)^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{4}(-1)^{j}\left(\bar{F}^{gg}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right)^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}(D_{0}^{2}),

(181a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}&=\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\Bigl[\bar{F}^{bb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{bg}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})-\bar{F}^{bb}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{bg}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})+\bar{F}^{gg}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\\
&\qquad\qquad-\bar{F}^{gg}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})+\bar{F}^{gg}_{4}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{3}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})-\bar{F}^{gg}_{3}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{4}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\Bigr]D_{0}+\mathcal{O}(D_{0}^{3}),\end{split}

(181b)

respectively. To arrive at these expressions, we have used the
C_{2z}
constraint in Eq. (
81
).

We first observe that, in both cases,
\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}=0
for
D_{0}=0
and, hence, the energy (
179
) is simply minimized when
\sin\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}=0
, i.e., no mixing between the graphene and TBG bands occurs. This is expected since the presence of
\sigma_{h}
implies that the order parameters have to be either even (intra-system) or odd (inter-system) under
\sigma_{h}
, prohibiting mixing between the bands.

When
D_{0}\neq 0
, the system can immediately gain energy by allowing for non-zero
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}
:
\Delta E^{\text{HF}}
in Eq. (
179
) is minimized by
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{0}
obeying
\tan 2\varphi^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}/\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}
with minimal energy given by

\Delta E^{\text{HF}}_{0}=\text{min}_{\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\Delta E^{\text{HF}}[\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}}\left(\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\sqrt{\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}+\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}\right)\leq 0.

(182)

As long as
\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}\neq 0
, this lowers the energy, which shows that the system will exhibit hybridization between the bands for
D_{0}\neq 0
as described by the two generators in Eq. (
177a
), while those in Eq. (
177b
) are disfavored energetically by the interactions. While this only holds for
w_{0}=0
, we still expect the generators in Eq. (
177a
) to be dominant in an extended region of finite
w_{0}
. As we will see shortly, this is confirmed by our numerics.

To obtain the scaling of the energy gain with (small)
D_{0}
, we expand Eq. (
182
) in
\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}\propto D_{0}
, which yields

\Delta E^{\text{HF}}_{0}\propto-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}}\frac{\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}{4\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\propto-g_{3}V_{\mathcal{A}}D_{0}^{2},

(183)

where we indicated that the result will be proportional to the area
V_{\mathcal{A}}\propto(\Delta k)^{2}
(our small parameter) and the displacement field squared; the constant
g_{1}>0
contains all the non-universal properties and itself depends on
D_{0}
and
w_{0}
.

Which of these two remaining generators in Eq. (
177a
) (or which linear combination) is preferred by the system is the
only
aspect that depends on the microscopic details of the form factors; it is not possible to say which of the
\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}
in Eqs. (
187b
) and (
181b
) is dominant at a given
\boldsymbol{k}
without computing the form factors in the microscopic model. The associated mixing of the bands for these two cases are readily evaluated from Eq. (
166
). We get

Q^{\text{b}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}=2\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}=\sqrt{2}i\sigma_{z}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})+\sqrt{2}\sigma_{0}(i\eta_{x}\pm\eta_{y}),\quad\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}

(184)

for the first term in Eq. (
177a
). This means that the graphene-like band with positive (negative) energy in the valley that is closer to the Fermi level mixes with the TBG-like band of both valleys that are also at positive (negative) energies.

For the second generator in Eq. (
177a
), we find a mixing matrix of the form

Q^{\text{b}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}=\sqrt{2}i\sigma_{y}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})-\sqrt{2}i\sigma_{x}(i\eta_{x}\pm\eta_{y}),\quad\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}.

(185)

Instead, we here get that the graphene-like band with positive (negative) energy in the valley that is closer to the Fermi level mixes with the TBG-like bands of both valleys that are at negative (positive) energies. In our numerical HF computations, we find very good agreement with the mixing matrix in Eq. (
185
), see Fig.
17
, even at the moderately large value of
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.73
.

The energetic discussion can be done in a similar way for the IVC
-
state and we find quite similar structures: first, we get
\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}=0
for
w_{0}=0
for the two generators in Eq. (
178b
) and, thus, no energetic gain via mixing. Second, this is different for those in Eq. (
178a
): the first one leads to

\displaystyle\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}

\displaystyle=4\left(2\epsilon_{(g,+),+}(\boldsymbol{k})+\sum_{\eta=\pm}\eta\epsilon_{(b,+),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})\right)+\frac{8}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\bar{F}^{bb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right)^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{4}(-1)^{j}\left(\bar{F}^{gg}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right)^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}(D_{0}^{2}),

(186a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}&=\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\Bigl[\bar{F}^{bb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{bg}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})-\bar{F}^{bb}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{bg}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})+\bar{F}^{gg}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\\
&\qquad\qquad-\bar{F}^{gg}_{1}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{2}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})+\bar{F}^{gg}_{4}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{3}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})-\bar{F}^{gg}_{3}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{4}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\Bigr]D_{0}+\mathcal{O}(D_{0}^{3}),\end{split}

(186b)

while the second one is associated with the coefficients

\displaystyle\alpha_{\boldsymbol{k}}

\displaystyle=4\left(2\epsilon_{(g,+),+}(\boldsymbol{k})-\sum_{\eta=\pm}\eta\epsilon_{(b,+),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})\right)+\frac{8}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\bar{F}^{bb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right)^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{4}(-1)^{j}\left(\bar{F}^{gg}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right)^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}(D_{0}^{2}),

(187a)

\displaystyle\beta_{\boldsymbol{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\left[\sum_{j=1}^{4}\bar{F}^{gg}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})-\sum_{j=1}^{2}\bar{F}^{bb}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{bg}_{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\right]D_{0}+\mathcal{O}(D_{0}^{3})

(187b)

in Eq. (
179
). As before, while
D_{0}=0
does not lead to mixing, the bands start to mix at non-zero
D_{0}
; this mixing is described by the two generators in Eq. (
178a
) and which of the two dominates depends on microscopic details.
The nature of the mixing depends on the generator. For the first one in Eq. (
178a
), the mixing matrix reads as

2\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}=\sqrt{2}i\sigma_{x}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})+\sqrt{2}\sigma_{0}(\eta_{y}\pm i\eta_{x}),\quad\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}.

(188)

This means that the lower/upper graphene-like band in the valley that is at low energies hybridizes with the upper/lower (lower/upper) TBG-like band of the same (opposite) valley. For the second generator in Eq. (
178a
), we get instead

2\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}=\sqrt{2}i\sigma_{z}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})+\sqrt{2}i\sigma_{y}(\eta_{y}\pm i\eta_{x}),\quad\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm},

(189)

i.e., the lower/upper graphene-like band in the valley that is at low energies hybridizes with the upper/lower (lower/upper) TBG-like band of the opposite (same) valley.

Comparison of Eqs. (
181
) and (
186
) [the same holds for Eqs. (
180
) and (
187
)] shows that the corresponding prefactors and, hence, the energies are identical to leading order in
D_{0}
(note the subleading corrections due to the kinetic term). Since our numerics has identified the mixing matrix in Eq. (
185
) to be realized in the system for the IVC
+
and the parameters in Fig.
17
, we thus expect Eq. (
188
) to dominate for the IVC
-
; this is indeed confirmed by Fig.
17
. Another consequence of this observation is that the leading (in
D_{0}
and
w_{0}
) correction to the energy of the IVC
-
is identical to that of the IVC
+
, given by
-g_{1}V_{\mathcal{A}}D_{0}^{2}
. As we have seen above, further corrections to higher orders in
D_{0}
(e.g., due to the kinetic terms) are different for these two IVC states. These are not included in Table
3
.

We next continue with the
spin polarized
state,
Q^{\text{b}}=\sigma_{0}\eta_{0}s_{3}
. Since the valley U(1)
v
symmetry is preserved,
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
has to be diagonal in valley space. We can write

\left(U_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=s_{0}\sigma_{0}\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}e^{i\sum_{j=1}^{3}\varphi^{(j)}_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\zeta_{j}}.

(190)

While this form of
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
automatically preserves all unitary symmetries in Table
5
(except for
P
, which, however, is broken anyway for
D_{0}\neq 0
), it only preserves
C_{2z}\Theta
if
\varphi^{(1)}=\varphi^{(3)}=0
and we are left with

\left(U_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=s_{0}\sigma_{0}\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}e^{i\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\zeta_{2}}

(191)

with the additional constraint
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=\varphi_{-\boldsymbol{k},-\eta}
, which comes from
\Theta
(or, equivalently,
C_{2z}
).
Our task now it to determine the real-valued function
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}
such that the energy is minimized. We expect the mixing to be the strongest (if at all present) around the K and K
′
points for valley
\eta=+
and
\eta=-
, respectively, and, thus, assume that
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\neq 0
only in the region
\mathcal{A}_{\eta}
and expand in its area leading to Eq. (
146
). The energy change due to
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}\neq\pm\mathbbm{1}
in Eq. (
191
) for the spin polarized state is found to be

\displaystyle\Delta E[\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\eta}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}\left(a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\sin^{2}\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}+b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\sin\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\cos\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\right)

(192)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}}\left(a_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sin^{2}\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}+b_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sin\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}\cos\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}\right),\qquad a_{\boldsymbol{k}}:=\sum_{\eta}a_{\eta\boldsymbol{k},\eta},\quad b_{\boldsymbol{k}}:=\sum_{\eta}b_{\eta\boldsymbol{k},\eta},

(193)

where we used the constraint
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=\varphi_{-\boldsymbol{k},-\eta}
in the second line.
Here and in all of the following computations of this appendix, we focus on terms that do not vanish for
w_{0}=0
. This is motivated by the fact that the final energy gain will already be of order
D_{0}^{2}
and so further corrections of order
w_{0}
of its prefactor will be subleading. We find

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{\boldsymbol{k}}&=2\sum_{p=\pm}p\left(\epsilon_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})-\epsilon_{(b,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})\right)\\
&+\frac{4}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\Bigl[(\bar{F}^{bb}_{1,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{bb}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{gg}_{1,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}^{gg}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\Bigr],\\
&-\frac{4}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\Bigl[(\bar{F}^{bg}_{1,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{gb}_{1,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{bg}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}^{gb}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\Bigr]D_{0}^{2}\end{split}

(194a)

\displaystyle b_{\boldsymbol{k}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{8}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\sum_{j=1,2}\Bigl[\bar{F}^{bb}_{j,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{bg}_{j,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})-\bar{F}^{gg}_{j,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{j,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\Bigr]D_{0}.

(194b)

We see that
b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=0
if
D_{0}=0
while
a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}>0
. So
\sin\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=0
minimizes the energy and, hence, there is no mixing between the bands for
D_{0}=0
, as expected by symmetry. However, any arbitrarily small non-zero
D_{0}
leads to
b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\neq 0
and the energy is lowered when the bands hybridize: Eq. (
192
) is minimized by
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}^{0}
obeying
\tan 2\varphi^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=-b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}/a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}
and with energy change

\text{min}_{\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}}\Delta E[\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}}\left(a_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\sqrt{a_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}+b_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}\right)\leq 0.

(195)

Since
b_{\boldsymbol{k}}\propto D_{0}
, the energy correction scales as
-g_{1}V_{\mathcal{A}}D_{0}^{2}
, as indicated schematically in Table
3
.

Valley polarized.
This state also preserves the
C_{2z}\Theta
and valley U(1)
v
symmetries, such that
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is again of the form of Eq. (
191
); this time, however, without the constraint
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=\varphi_{-\boldsymbol{k},-\eta}
as
C_{2z}
and
\Theta
are both broken individually. The energy change is of the form of Eq. (
192
) with coefficients given by (again, only keeping terms to zeroth order in
w_{0}
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}&=2\left(\epsilon_{(g,+),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})-\epsilon_{(b,+),\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})\right)\\
&+\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\Bigl[(\bar{F}^{bb}_{1,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{bb}_{2,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{gg}_{1,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}^{gg}_{2,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\Bigr],\\
&-\frac{2}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\Bigl[(\bar{F}^{bg}_{1,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{gb}_{1,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{bg}_{2,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}^{gb}_{2,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\Bigr]D_{0}^{2}\end{split}

(196a)

\displaystyle b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\sum_{j=1,2}\Bigl[\bar{F}^{bb}_{j,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{bg}_{j,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})-\bar{F}^{gg}_{j,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{j,\eta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\Bigr]D_{0}.

(196b)

Minimization yields

\text{min}_{\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}}\Delta E[\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\eta}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}}\left(a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}^{2}-\sqrt{a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}^{2}+b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}^{2}}\right)\leq 0.

(197)

It is easy to see that
a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=a_{-\boldsymbol{k},-\eta}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=b_{-\boldsymbol{k},-\eta}
, following from
C_{2z}
symmetry of the form factors and dispersion, and that
a_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{k}}
in Eq. (
194
) are given by
a_{\boldsymbol{k}}=2a_{\boldsymbol{k},+}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{k}}=2b_{\boldsymbol{k},+}
. As such the energy gain is the same for the spin and for the valley polarized states at
w_{0}=0
.

Next, we discuss the
quantum Hall state
SLP
-
. While this state also preserves valley U(1)
v
symmetry and spin rotations, it breaks the
\boldsymbol{k}
-space-local
C_{2z}\Theta
symmetry. As such, the transformation has to be of the more general form in Eq. (
190
), involving three generators,
\zeta_{x,y,y}
. Due to the preserved
C_{2z}
symmetry, we have
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}^{(j)}=\varphi_{-\boldsymbol{k},-}^{(j)}
. Exactly as before, general energetic reasoning leads to
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}^{(j)}=0
if
\boldsymbol{k}\notin\mathcal{A}_{\eta}
.
Instead of optimizing all three associated angles
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}^{(j)}
simultaneously, let us first investigate them one at a time: first of all,
j=3
leads to
\Delta E^{\text{HF}}=0
(for
w_{0}=0
) in Eq. (
146
). The generator
\zeta_{x}
does lead to non-zero
\Delta E^{\text{HF}}
, which is found to be of the form of Eq. (
192
); this time with
b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=0
and
a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}
as given in Eq. (
196a
). Since
a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}>0
, at least for sufficiently small
D_{0}
, the system does not gain any energy as a consequence of hybridization described by
(U_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}e^{i\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\zeta_{x}}
. The only generator left is
j=2
, i.e.,
(U_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}e^{i\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\zeta_{y}}
; in fact, this one allows for a reduction of energy and we obtain, to order
w_{0}^{0}
, exactly the same of
a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}
as defined in Eq. (
196
). In order to take into account the
C_{2z}
constraint,
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=\varphi_{-\boldsymbol{k},-\eta}
, we again simplify from Eq. (
192
) to Eq. (
193
). Upon noting, as above, that
a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=a_{-\boldsymbol{k},-\eta}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=b_{-\boldsymbol{k},-\eta}
, we have
a_{\boldsymbol{k}}=2a_{\boldsymbol{k},+}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{k}}=2b_{\boldsymbol{k},+}
and the energy reduction due to hybridization of the SLP
-
is the same as that of the VP state (at
w_{0}=0
).

Upon noting that
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=i\sigma_{0}s_{0}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})
,
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
, in Eq. (
166
) for our
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
studied here, it is easy to compute the mixing matrix:

Q^{\text{b}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}=i(\sigma_{y}+\sigma_{z})(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z}),\qquad\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm},

(198)

i.e., both graphene-like bands in the valley that is at low energies mixes with both TBG-like bands in the same valley. This agrees well with the correlator near the K point of the SLP
-
state obtained in HF, see Fig.
17
.

To continue with the
valley Hall state
SLP
+
, we note that
C_{2z}\Theta
is also broken in this state, leading again to the general form of
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
given in Eq. (
190
) with three generators. This time
\Theta
(rather than
C_{2z}
) is preserved and we have
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}^{(j)}=(-1)^{j}\varphi_{-\boldsymbol{k},-}^{(j)}
. As above, we study first each generator,
j=1,2,3
, separately and note that
j=1
and
j=3
do not allow to lower the energy (again, for
w_{0}=0
and not too large values of
D_{0}
). However,
j=2
does allow to lower the energy via hybridization for any non-zero
D_{0}
. Writing
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}=\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},+}^{(2)}
, the energy gain is of the form (
193
) with prefactors

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{\boldsymbol{k}}&=2\sum_{p=\pm}p\left(\epsilon_{(g,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})-\epsilon_{(b,p),+}(\boldsymbol{k})\right)\\
&+\frac{4}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\Bigl[(\bar{F}^{bb}_{1,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{bb}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{gg}_{1,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}^{gg}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\Bigr],\\
&-\frac{4}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\Bigl[(\bar{F}^{bg}_{1,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{gb}_{1,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}^{bg}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-(\bar{F}^{gb}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}\Bigr]D_{0}^{2}\\
&+\frac{32}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}}V(\boldsymbol{G})\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bar{F}_{4}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{G})\right)\left(\bar{F}^{bb}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})-\bar{F}^{gg}_{2,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})\right)D_{0}^{2}\end{split}

(199a)

\displaystyle b_{\boldsymbol{k}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{8}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}V(\boldsymbol{q})\sum_{j=1,2}\Bigl[\bar{F}^{bb}_{j,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{bg}_{j,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})-\bar{F}^{gg}_{j,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\bar{F}^{gb}_{j,+}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\Bigr]D_{0}.

(199b)

Comparison with the expressions for the VP and SLP
-
shows that the energy reduction due to hybridization is almost the same for the SLP
+
. The only difference is the additional Hartree term in
a_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, which is expected to be only a very small correction due to Eq. (
84
) and since it is higher order in
D_{0}
. As such, we have
g_{3}\simeq g_{1}
in Table
3
.

The corresponding
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=i\sigma_{0}s_{0}(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z})
,
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
, in Eq. (
166
) leads to the mixing contribution in
Q_{\boldsymbol{k}}

Q^{\text{b}}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sigma_{z}=i(\sigma_{y}\eta_{z}+\sigma_{z})(\eta_{0}\pm\eta_{z}),\qquad\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pm}.

(200)

This means that both the upper and the lower graphene-like bands in the valley that is at low energies mixes with both TBG-like bands in the same valley, in perfect agreement with Fig.
17
.

Finally, the situation is slightly more complex for the SSLP
-
state, as it also breaks SU(2) spin-rotation symmetry down to residual rotation along one spin axis (say
s_{z}
). Consequently, we will have to allow for six generators,

\left(U_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=\sigma_{0}\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}e^{i\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\varphi^{(j,0)}_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\zeta_{j}s_{0}+\varphi^{(j,3)}_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\zeta_{j}s_{3})},\qquad\varphi^{(j,a)}_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=\varphi^{(j,a)}_{-\boldsymbol{k},-\eta},

(201)

where the constraint comes from
C_{2z}
symmetry. Let us investigate every generator separately. First, one finds no change in energy for
\zeta_{3}s_{0}
and
\zeta_{3}s_{3}
. In all other cases, the energy change is again of the form (
192
). For
\zeta_{1,2}s_{3}
and
\zeta_{1}s_{0}
we get
b_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=0
and
a_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}>0
(for sufficiently small
D_{0}
) and, hence, no associated energetic gain. Hence, we are, again, only left with a single generator and
(U_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}=\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}e^{i\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}\zeta_{2}s_{0}}
with
\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k},\eta}=\varphi_{-\boldsymbol{k},\eta}
. The prefactors are given by Eq. (
196
) and we obtain the same energetic gain as for the SLP
-
(or VP and SP) state. As anticipated in Appendix
F.3
, this mixing induces a gap for the SSLP
-
state as well, as also seen in our HF numerics, despite not explicitly ordering in the graphene-like bands (for small
D_{0}
).

F.5
Hund’s Coupling

In the real system, SU(2)
{}_{+}\times
SU(2)
-
is broken down to SU(2)
s
. Keeping U(1)
v
as an exact symmetry, any spin-rotation invariant non-interacting Hamiltonian will automatically be invariant under SU(2)
{}_{+}\times
SU(2)
-
and, hence, the explicit breaking of this enhanced spin symmetry can only be captured by interaction terms. We refer to all such interaction terms collectively as “intervalley Hund’s interactions”.

In order to associate a sign of the Hund’s coupling with a member of a given Hund’s pair of states, we use the simple explicit form of the Hund’s interaction given in Eq. (
30
) of the main text. By evaluation their respective Hartree and Fock energies, we can express the contribution of this interaction to each state in Table
2
:

\begin{split}\Delta E_{\text{SP}}&=-\Delta E_{\text{SVP}}=\frac{2J_{H}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}}\left(\left[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bar{F}_{1}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})\right]^{2}-4\left[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bar{F}_{3}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})\right]^{2}D_{0}^{4}\right)\\
\Delta E_{\text{IVC}_{-}}&=-3\Delta E_{\text{SIVC}_{-}}=-\frac{3J_{H}}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\left[(\bar{F}_{1}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}_{2}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-4(\bar{F}_{3}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}D_{0}^{4}-4(\bar{F}_{4}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}D_{0}^{4}\right.\\
&\left.+4(\bar{F}_{5}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}w_{0}^{2}+4(\bar{F}_{6}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}w_{0}^{2}-(\bar{F}_{7}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}w_{0}^{2}D_{0}^{4}-(\bar{F}_{8}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}w_{0}^{2}D_{0}^{4}\right]\\
\Delta E_{\text{IVC}_{+}}&=-3\Delta E_{\text{SIVC}_{+}}=-\frac{3J_{H}}{2N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\left[(\bar{F}_{1}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}+(\bar{F}_{2}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}-4(\bar{F}_{3}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}D_{0}^{4}-4(\bar{F}_{4}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}D_{0}^{4}\right.\\
&\left.-4(\bar{F}_{5}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}w_{0}^{2}-4(\bar{F}_{6}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}w_{0}^{2}+(\bar{F}_{7}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}w_{0}^{2}D_{0}^{4}+(\bar{F}_{8}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}))^{2}w_{0}^{2}D_{0}^{4}\right]\\
\Delta E_{\text{SSLP}_{\pm}}&=\mp\frac{2J_{H}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}}\left(\left[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bar{F}_{2}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})\right]^{2}-4\left[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bar{F}_{4}^{bb}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{G})\right]^{2}D_{0}^{4}\right)\\
\Delta E_{\text{SLP}_{\pm}}&=0\\
\Delta E_{\text{VP}}&=0\end{split}

(202)

The form of the energies in the chiral-decoupled limit,
w_{0}=D_{0}=0
, is indicated in the last two columns in Table
3
. Their signs can be found in the last column of Table
2
.

By construction of the order parameters in Table
2
, we see that a state and its Hund’s partner have an energy contributions from
J_{H}
of opposite sign. Furthermore, all states that are their own Hund’s partners, are not affected energetically to linear order in
J_{H}
.

Appendix G
Superconducting instabilities

G.1
Fluctuation-induced superconductivity

Finally, in this appendix we provide more details on how we determine the leading superconducting instabilities in Sec.
VII.2
that result from fluctuations of the candidate particle-hole orders of MSTG identified in this work. Since the applied formalism is closely connected to Ref.
Samajdar and Scheurer 2020
, we will be brief and focus on the novel aspects relevant here—the spinless valley time-reversal symmetry
\widetilde{\Theta}
of some of the states in Table
2
and what happens in the absence of electron-phonon coupling—and illustrate the formalism explicitly with a minimal model for the IVC
-
.

Integrating out the bosonic fields
\phi_{q}^{j}
in the action
\mathcal{S}
in Eq. (
42
) leads to an effective electron-electron interaction. Projecting this interaction to the electronic degrees of freedom,
f_{k;\eta,s}
, with band energies
\epsilon_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{k})
, that are closest to the chemical potential for each momentum
\boldsymbol{k}
, we perform a decoupling in the Cooper channel (focusing on inter-valley pairing which is expected to be dominant), with Hubbard-Stratonovich fields
\Delta^{\eta}_{s,s^{\prime}}
, and obtain the saddle-point equations
Samajdar and Scheurer 2020

\Delta_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\eta}(k)=-\sum_{\eta^{\prime}}\int_{k^{\prime}}\frac{\mathcal{V}^{s,s^{\prime}}_{\bar{s},\bar{s}^{\prime}}(k,\eta;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})}{\omega_{n^{\prime}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}_{\eta^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})}\Delta_{\bar{s}^{\prime},\bar{s}}^{\eta^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}).

(203)

Here the interaction matrix elements have the form
\mathcal{V}^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{s_{3},s_{4}}(k,\eta;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=\mathcal{W}^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{s_{3},s_{4}}(k,\eta;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})+\Delta\mathcal{V}^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{s_{3},s_{4}}(k,\eta;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
, where the first part is the SU(2)
{}_{+}\times
SU(2)
-
symmetric contribution of the phonons from
S_{\text{phonon}}
and the second is a result of the particle-hole fluctuations,

\Delta\mathcal{V}^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{s_{3},s_{4}}(k,\eta;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{2}\Lambda^{j}_{-\eta,s_{2};-\eta^{\prime},s_{3}}(-\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})[\chi(k-k^{\prime})]_{j,j^{\prime}}\Lambda^{j^{\prime}}_{\eta,s_{1};\eta^{\prime},s_{4}}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}),

(204)

where
\Lambda^{j}_{\eta,s;\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}):=\braket{u_{\eta,s}(\boldsymbol{k})|\lambda^{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})|u_{\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})}
are the matrix elements of the coupling vertices
\lambda^{j}
in Eq. (
44
) with the respect to the wavefunctions
u_{\eta,s}(\boldsymbol{k})
of the low-energy fermions
f_{k;\eta,s}
.

Since the system has SU(2)
s
symmetry, we can always decompose pairing into singlet and triplet, which correspond to
\Delta_{s,s^{\prime}}(k)=(is_{y})_{s,s^{\prime}}\gamma_{k}^{\eta}
,
\gamma_{k}^{\eta}=\gamma_{-k}^{-\eta}
, and
