\Delta_{s,s^{\prime}}(k)=(is_{y}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{s})_{s,s^{\prime}}\Gamma_{k}^{\eta}
,
\Gamma_{k}^{\eta}=-\Gamma_{-k}^{-\eta}
, respectively. Their respective saddle-point equations immediately follow from Eq. (
203
) and read as

\gamma_{k}^{+}=\int_{k^{\prime}}\frac{M^{s}_{k,k^{\prime}}}{\omega_{n^{\prime}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}_{+}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})}\gamma_{k^{\prime}}^{+},\quad M^{s}_{k,k^{\prime}}=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{s,s^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}}ss^{\prime}\,\mathcal{V}^{s,-s}_{-s^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(\eta k,\eta;\eta^{\prime}k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}),

(205a)

and

\Gamma_{k}^{+}=\int_{k^{\prime}}\frac{M^{t}_{k,k^{\prime}}}{\omega_{n^{\prime}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}_{+}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})}\Gamma_{k^{\prime}}^{+},\quad M^{t}_{k,k^{\prime}}=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{s,s^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\eta\eta^{\prime}\,\mathcal{V}^{s,-s}_{-s^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(\eta k,\eta;\eta^{\prime}k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}),

(205b)

respectively.

To make progress, we need to take into account time-reversal symmetry. To begin with
\widetilde{\Theta}
, we have
\widetilde{\Theta}\ket{u_{\eta,s}(\boldsymbol{k})}=\eta\ket{u_{-\eta,-s}(-\boldsymbol{k})}
(technically, we are here using
\widetilde{\Theta}=s_{x}\eta_{y}\mathcal{K}
but this can equally well be used as a definition of
\widetilde{\Theta}
in Table
2
). For any set of bosonic modes,
\phi_{q}^{j}
that are all even (
\tilde{t}_{\phi}=+
) or odd (
\tilde{t}_{\phi}=-
) under
\widetilde{\Theta}
, we further have
\widetilde{\Theta}\lambda^{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\widetilde{\Theta}^{\dagger}=\tilde{t}_{\phi}\,\lambda^{j}(-\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
and, hence,

\Lambda^{j}_{\eta,s;\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})=\eta\,\eta^{\prime}\,\tilde{t}_{\phi}\,\left[\Lambda^{j}_{-\eta,-s;-\eta^{\prime},-s^{\prime}}(-\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\right]^{*}.

(206)

Similarly, we have
\Lambda^{j}_{\eta,s;\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})=\sigma\,\sigma^{\prime}\,t_{\phi}\,\left[\Lambda^{j}_{-\eta,-s;-\eta^{\prime},-s^{\prime}}(-\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\right]^{*}
for bosonic modes even (
t_{\phi}=+
) or odd (
t_{\phi}=-
) under
\Theta_{s}

Scheurer 2016
;
Samajdar and Scheurer 2020
.
This allows us to rewrite the relevant SU(2)
{}_{+}\times
SU(2)
-
-symmetry breaking part,
\Delta M^{s,t}
, in
M^{s,t}=M_{0}^{s,t}+\Delta M^{s,t}
(with
M_{0}^{s,t}
related to
\mathcal{W}
and phonons) as

\left(\Delta M^{s}\right)_{k,k^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{8}\tilde{t}_{\phi}\sum_{s,s^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}}ss^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}\mathcal{F}_{k,\eta,s;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}},\qquad\left(\Delta M^{t}\right)_{k,k^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{8}\tilde{t}_{\phi}\sum_{s,s^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{k,\eta,s;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}},

(207)

when applying
\widetilde{\Theta}
. We here introduced

\mathcal{F}_{k,\eta,s;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}=\left[\Lambda^{j}_{\eta,s;\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(\eta\boldsymbol{k},\eta^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\right]^{*}[\chi(\eta k-\eta^{\prime}k^{\prime})]_{j,j^{\prime}}\Lambda^{j^{\prime}}_{\eta,s;\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(\eta\boldsymbol{k},\eta^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\geq 0,

(208)

which is non-negative, since
\chi
has to be positive definite due to stability
Scheurer 2016
;
Samajdar and Scheurer 2020
. For conventional time-reversal symmetry,
\Theta_{s}
, singlet and triplet switch their role
Samajdar and Scheurer 2020

\left(\Delta M^{s}\right)_{k,k^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{8}t_{\phi}\sum_{s,s^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{k,\eta,s;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}},\qquad\left(\Delta M^{t}\right)_{k,k^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{8}t_{\phi}\sum_{s,s^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}}ss^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}\mathcal{F}_{k,\eta,s;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}.

(209)

Upon writing
\Delta_{s}(k)=\gamma_{k}^{+}/\sqrt{\omega_{n}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}_{+}(\boldsymbol{k})}
and
\Delta_{t}(k)=\Gamma_{k}^{+}/\sqrt{\omega_{n}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}_{+}(\boldsymbol{k})}
, we can recast solving the saddle-point equations in Eq. (
205b
) as solving for the largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{t,s}(T)
of the symmetric matrix
(\widetilde{M}^{s,t})_{k,k^{\prime}}:=M^{t,s}_{k,k^{\prime}}/\sqrt{(\omega_{n}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}_{+}(\boldsymbol{k}))(\omega_{n^{\prime}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}_{+}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}))}
as a function of temperature
T
. The critical temperature,
T_{c}^{s,t}
, in the two channels corresponds to
\lambda_{t,s}(T_{c}^{s,t})=1
.
Defining
(\Delta\widetilde{M}^{s,t})_{k,k^{\prime}}:=\Delta M^{t,s}_{k,k^{\prime}}/\sqrt{(\omega_{n}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}_{+}(\boldsymbol{k}))(\omega_{n^{\prime}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}_{+}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}))}
, we immediately see from Eqs. (
207
) and (
209
), that
(\Delta\widetilde{M}^{s})_{k,k^{\prime}}\geq(\Delta\widetilde{M}^{t})_{k,k^{\prime}}
if
t_{\phi}=+
or
\tilde{t}_{\phi}=-
and
(\Delta\widetilde{M}^{t})_{k,k^{\prime}}\geq(\Delta\widetilde{M}^{t})_{k,k^{\prime}}
if
t_{\phi}=-
or
\tilde{t}_{\phi}=+
.

Let us first discuss the situation without electron-phonon coupling and, hence,
\widetilde{M}^{s,t}=\Delta\widetilde{M}^{s,t}
. As follows from the Perron-Frobenius theorem, given two square matrices
A
and
B
of equal size with
A_{ij}\geq|B_{ij}|
and
A
being irreducible
2
2

2

This means that its adjacency graph is strongly connected. In our case, it corresponds to the situation that one can scatter between any pair of momenta via some number of virtual states; at least for
D_{0}\neq 0
, this is generically expected to be the case.
, the largest eigenvalue of
A
(the “Perron root”) is larger than the magnitude of the largest eigenvalue of
B
. We then have
\lambda_{s}(T)\geq\lambda_{t}(T)
and, hence,
T_{c}^{s}\geq T_{c}^{t}
if
t_{\phi}=+
;
this means that singlet will generically dominate over triplet. Similarly, if

\tilde{t}_{\phi}=+
, we get
\lambda_{t}(T)\geq\lambda_{s}(T)
leading to
T_{c}^{t}\geq T_{c}^{s}
, which means that triplet will dominate. Note that if the fluctuations have a well-defined behavior under both
\Theta_{s}
and
\widetilde{\Theta}
and we further have
t_{\phi}=\tilde{t}_{\phi}
, Eqs. (
207
) and (
209
) imply that
\Delta M^{s}=\Delta M^{t}
, i.e., singlet and triplet remain degenerate.

In the scenario proposed in Ref.
Samajdar and Scheurer 2020
, where electron-phonon coupling yields an important contribution in stabilizing superconductivity but predominantly in the SU(2)
{}_{+}\times
SU(2)
-
-symmetric channel, we treat the symmetry-breaking
\Delta\widetilde{M}^{s,t}
as a small perturbation to the SU(2)
{}_{+}\times
SU(2)
-
-symmetric
\widetilde{M}^{s,t}_{0}:=\widetilde{M}^{s,t}-\Delta\widetilde{M}^{s,t}
associated with the phonons. As follows from the analysis of Ref.
Scheurer 2016
, we must have
\Delta^{0}_{t,s}(k)>0
for the unperturbed saddle-point solutions, i.e., leading eigenstates of
\widetilde{M}^{s,t}_{0}
. As such, it is clear that the leading (first-order) correction
\delta\lambda_{s,t}=\int_{k,k^{\prime}}\Delta^{0}_{t,s}(k)(\Delta\widetilde{M}^{s,t})_{k,k^{\prime}}\Delta^{0}_{t,s}(k^{\prime})
is larger for singlet (triplet), which then dominates, if
t_{\phi}=+
(
t_{\phi}=-
) or
\tilde{t}_{\phi}=-
(
\tilde{t}_{\phi}=+
).

We illustrate these statements more explicitly for the IVC
-
state, which is odd under
\Theta_{s}
and even under
\widetilde{\Theta}
, i.e., characterized by
t_{\phi}=-
and
\tilde{t}_{\phi}=+
. As already mentioned in Sec.
VII.2
, a minimal description is given by
[\chi(i\Omega_{n},\boldsymbol{q})]_{j,j^{\prime}}=\chi_{0}\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}/(\Omega_{n}^{2}+c^{2}\boldsymbol{q}^{2}+\xi^{-2})
,
j=1,2
, and
\lambda^{j}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})=s_{0}\eta_{j}f_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
, where
f_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
is a matrix in band space with
f^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=f^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}}=-f^{T}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}}
. This leads to

\mathcal{F}_{k,\eta,s;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}=\frac{2\chi_{0}\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta,-\eta^{\prime}}}{\Omega_{n}^{2}+c^{2}\boldsymbol{q}^{2}+\xi^{-2}}\left|\braket{u_{\eta,s}(\boldsymbol{k})|f_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|u_{\eta,s}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})}\right|^{2}\geq 0.

(210)

So only matrix elements with
ss^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}=-1
are non-zero such that
\Delta M^{s,t}
are identical in Eqs. (
207
) and (
209
) for
t_{\phi}=-
and
\tilde{t}_{\phi}=+
. We, thus, consistently see that IVC
-
fluctuations will favor the triplet state.

Finally, consider the SLP
-
state, which is odd under both
\Theta_{s}
and
\widetilde{\Theta}
and, thus, has
t_{\phi}=\tilde{t}_{\phi}=-
. To be consistent with the analysis above, fluctuations of this state must affect singlet and triplet in exactly the same way. This can also be easily seen explicitly by noting that its coupling vertex
\lambda
in Eq. (
44
) cannot couple different valleys or spin species and, hence,
\mathcal{F}_{k,\eta,s;k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\propto\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}
. Consequently, we get
\Delta M^{s}=\Delta M^{t}
in Eqs. (
207
) and (
209
).

G.2
Order parameter in the polarized phase

Finally, we also illustrate the spin-structure of the superconducting order parameter in the presence of flavor polarization, relevant for
\nu
with
2\lesssim|\nu|\lesssim 3
. For both SP-based and SVP-based band resetting, the superconducting order parameter in the TBG-like bands has the form

H_{\text{SC,p}}^{b}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,\psi_{\boldsymbol{k};+}^{\dagger}\psi_{-\boldsymbol{k};-}^{\dagger}+\text{H.c.},

(211)

where
\psi_{\boldsymbol{k};\pm}
are the electronic operators of valley
\eta=\pm
in the polarized TBG-like bands which are closest to the Fermi level for each
\boldsymbol{k}
. In principle, there are also finite band-off-diagonal matrix elements. However, since we are only interested in the predominant spin-structure of the superconductor, we do not need to take them into account. Note that, if the band resetting also involves intervalley coherent order, such as in Fig.
7
(c,d), the labels
\pm
of
\psi_{\boldsymbol{k},\pm}
have to be understood as combinations of the two valleys; the spin structure we discuss here will not be affected by it.

To transform back to the physical spin (
s=\uparrow,\downarrow
) basis, we write
\psi_{\boldsymbol{k};\eta}=\sum_{s}(u^{*}_{\boldsymbol{k};\eta})_{s}f_{\boldsymbol{k};\eta,s}
, where the two-component spinor,
u_{\boldsymbol{k};\eta}
, encodes the nature of the flavor polarization. The singlet,
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{s}
, and triplet,
\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, component of the order parameter, coupling as
H_{\text{SC,p}}^{b}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}f_{\boldsymbol{k};+,s}^{\dagger}\left((s_{0}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{s}+\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}})is_{y}\right))_{s,s^{\prime}}f_{-\boldsymbol{k};-,s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}+\text{H.c.}
, then follow from

\left((s_{0}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{s}+\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}})is_{y}\right))_{s,s^{\prime}}=(u_{\boldsymbol{k};+})_{s}(u_{-\boldsymbol{k};-})_{s^{\prime}}.

(212)

For the case of SP, where the spin polarization is in the same direction in the two valleys, say along the
s_{z}
direction, we have

(u_{\boldsymbol{k};\pm})_{s}=\delta_{s,\uparrow},\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{s}=0,\,\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}(1,i,0)^{T}/2,

(213)

corresponding to non-unitary triplet pairing. A Zeemann field,
\boldsymbol{B}_{Z}
, will not affect the polarization nor the superconductor (except for pinning the direction of their magnetizations).

For the SVP-type of polarization, we have instead (choosing the polarization along the
s_{x}
axis)

u_{\boldsymbol{k};\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\pm 1\\
1\end{pmatrix}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{s}=\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}/2,\,\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}(1,0,0)^{T}/2,

(214)

leading to an equal admixture of singlet and triplet pairing
Scheurer and Samajdar 2020
.
Applying
\boldsymbol{B}_{Z}
, will pin the orientation of the spin to be orthogonal to it and induce a canting of angle
\theta
that increases with
|\boldsymbol{B}_{Z}|
. For
\boldsymbol{B}_{Z}
along the
s_{z}
direction, we thus get

u_{\boldsymbol{k};\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1+\sin\theta)}}\begin{pmatrix}\pm(1+\sin\theta)\\
\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{s}=\cos\theta\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}/2,\,\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\Delta_{\boldsymbol{k}}(1,i\sin\theta,0)^{T}/2.

(215)

So we see that this state continuously transforms into the non-unitary triplet when applying a magnetic field.
We note the behavior in magnetic field of these pairing states—the non-unitary triplet and singlet-triplet admixed phases—is the same in the absence of polarization, as shown in Ref.
Scheurer and Samajdar 2020
.

Appendix H
WZW terms in MSTG

In this appendix we will give more details on how we reached the conclusions in Sec.
VII.3
. We will begin as in the main text with
|\nu|=0
. In this case, as in the main text, the low-energy physics is captured by

H^{\text{D}}_{0}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}\psi^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left[\gamma_{1}q_{x}+\gamma_{2}q_{y}\right]\psi^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{q}},\qquad\gamma_{1}=\rho_{x}\mu_{z}s_{0},\quad\gamma_{2}=\rho_{y}\mu_{0}s_{0},

(216)

where we are using the notation for Pauli matrices defined in Table
6
. We will assume a pairing
\Delta
with 2 real components and an insulating order parameter
m_{j}
with three real components, incorporated as a Dirac masses,

H^{\text{D}}_{1}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{q},j}\psi^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{q}}m_{j}\psi^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\psi^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\Delta T\psi^{\dagger}_{-\boldsymbol{q}}+\text{H.c.}\right)

(217)

with
T=\mu_{x}s_{y}
. We want to consider all possible pairings
\Delta
and insulating states
m_{j}
such that when the Dirac fermions are integrated out of the action corresponding to
H^{\text{D}}_{0}+H^{\text{D}}_{1}
, a WZW term will be produced. One way to do this would be to consider which of the options classified in
Christos et al. 2020
survive projection to a minivalley-valley locked subspace. Alternatively, we can consider which orders are compatible with the conditions derived in
Christos et al. 2020
for the projected form of the Dirac action. We will choose the second way in this appendix. We will first consider the compatible pairings
\Delta
at
\nu=0
. At
\nu=0
, triplet pairing is excluded as we require SU(2)
s
spin symmetry to be intact before the bands acquire any ordering. A requirement for a pairing to be compatible with the existence of a WZW term is that it must satisfy the following conditions with the kinetic term’s
\gamma
matrices

\gamma_{i}\Delta T=-\Delta T\gamma_{i}^{T}\neq 0\qquad i=1,2.

(218)

We will additionally assume pairings which do not break moiré translational symmetry and intervalley pairing terms which preserve
U(1)_{v}
. We have also chosen a momentum independent
\Delta
. The only pairing compatible with all the above requirements is
\Delta=\mathbbm{1}
, transforming under the
A
representation of the point group. As mentioned in the main text, this is the same pairing considered for TBG in
Christos et al. 2020
projected to the minivalley-valley locked subspace. For this pairing, we can then ask what are the compatible 3-component order parameters
m_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
. The conditions the insulating states must satisfy with the pairing and
\gamma
matrices are:

\text{Tr}[\gamma_{i_{1}}\gamma_{i_{2}}m_{j_{1}}m_{j_{2}}m_{j_{3}}]\propto\epsilon_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}j_{1}j_{2}j_{3}}

(219)

m_{j}\Delta T=\Delta Tm_{j}^{T}\neq 0\qquad j=1,2,3

(220)

We find the only two options are a 3-component spin-Hall state
\rho_{z}\mu_{z}(s_{x},s_{y},s_{z})
and a 2-component moiré density wave state
\rho_{x}(\mu_{x},\mu_{y})
combined with a third component sublattice polarized state
\rho_{z}
.

Table 6
:
We explicitly list the notation we use to denote the Pauli matrices which act on the sublattice, minivalley, valley, and spin degrees of freedom used throughout the text in the second column, and the notation used in
Christos et al. 2020
in the third column.

Notation in this work

Notation in
Christos et al. 2020

Sublattice

\rho

\rho

Minivalley

\mu

\mu

Valley

\eta

\tau

Spin

s

\sigma

We can then consider the case at
|\nu|=2
.
As argued in the main text, the low-energy Dirac theory takes the same form as at
\nu=0
, see Eq. (
216
).

However, unlike the case at
\nu=0
, we no longer assume SU(2)
s
spin symmetry. It is simple to see the options which were compatible at
\nu=0
for singlet pairing are still compatible at
\nu=2
. On the other hand, the breaking of spin rotation symmetry allows for additional options at
|\nu|=2
which were not previously possible at
|\nu|=0
. It is easily verified that the triplet pairing
\Delta=\mu_{z}s_{z}
satisfies Eq. (
218
). We find two options which are compatible with this pairing. The first is the set of orders
\rho_{z}(s_{x},s_{y})
and
\rho_{z}\mu_{z}s_{z}
, corresponding to our SSLP
+
and SSLP
-
respectively. The second is
\rho_{x}(\mu_{x},\mu_{y})
and
\rho_{z}
, corresponding to a moiré density wave state and SLP
+
state respectively.
